Non-archimedean integration on totally disconnected spaces.
Francesco Baldassarri

Abstract
We work in the category 𝒞 ​ ℒ ​ ℳ k u {\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k} of [ 5 ] of separated complete bounded k k -linearly topologized modules over a complete linearly topologized ring k k and discuss duality on certain exact subcategories. We study topological and uniform structures on locally compact paracompact 0 0 -dimensional topological spaces X X , named t ​ d td -spaces in [ 11 ] and [ 17 ] , and the corresponding algebras 𝒞 ? ​ ( X , k ) {\mathscr{C}}_{?}(X,k) of continuous k k -valued functions, with a choice of support and uniformity conditions. We apply the previous duality theory to define and study the dual coalgebras 𝒟 ? ​ ( X , k ) {\mathscr{D}}_{?}(X,k) of k k -valued measures on X X . We then complete the picture by providing a direct definition of the various types of measures. In the case of X X a commutative t ​ d td -group G G the integration pairing provides perfect dualities of Hopf k k -algebras between 𝒞 unif ​ ( G , k ) ⟶ 𝒞 ⁡ ( G , k ) ​ and ​ 𝒟 acs ​ ( G , k ) ⟶ 𝒟 unif ​ ( G , k ) . {\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(G,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(G,k)\;\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\;{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(G,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(G,k)\;. We conclude the paper with the remarkable example of G = 𝔾 a ​ ( ℚ p ) G={\mathbb{G}}_{a}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}) and k = ℤ p k={\mathbb{Z}}_{p} , leading to the basic Fontaine ring 𝐀 inf = W ⁡ ( 𝔽 p ​ [ [ t 1 / p ∞ ] ] ^ ) = 𝒟 unif ​ ( ℚ p , ℤ p ) . {\bf A}_{\inf}={\rm W}\left(\widehat{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}[[t^{1/p^{\infty}}]]}\right)={\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\;. We discuss Fourier duality between 𝐀 inf {\bf A}_{\inf} and 𝒞 unif ​ ( ℚ p , ℤ p ) {\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}) and exhibit a remarkable Fréchet basis of 𝒞 unif ​ ( ℚ p , ℤ p ) {\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}) related to the classical binomial coefficients.



0
Conventions

A prime number
p
is fixed throughout this paper although it will only play a role in the applications of the last sections
9
to
11
.
We fix a universe
{\mathcal{U}}
and assume every object we deal with, except
{\mathcal{U}}
itself, is “small”,
i.e.
is an object of
{\mathcal{U}}
. In particular limits and colimits are understood to be “small”. Unless otherwise specified, a
ring
is meant to be commutative with 1. For any ring
R
,
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{R}
will denote the category of
R
-modules. It is a bicomplete abelian category. For
M\in{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{R}
and any small set
A
,
M^{A}
(resp.
M^{(A)}
) will denote the product (resp. sum) in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{R}
of the family
(M_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
with
M_{\alpha}=M
for any
\alpha\in A
.
A topological ring is
linearly topologized
if it admits a basis of neighborhoods of
0
consisting of ideals. If
R
is a linearly topologized ring, a topological
R
-module
M
is said to be
linearly topologized
if
M
admits a basis of open neighborhoods of
0
consisting of
R
-submodules.
The ring underlying the topological ring
R
will be denoted by
R^{\rm for}
; similarly, the
R^{\rm for}
-module underlying the topological
R
-module
M
, will be denoted by
M^{\rm for}
. Such care will often be waived unless there is a risk of confusion.

If
K
is a non-archimedean field, both the ring of integers
K^{\circ}
and the residue field
\widetilde{K}=K^{\circ}/K^{\circ\circ}
are linearly topologized rings, while
K
, unless its valuation is trivial, is only linearly topologized as a topological
K^{\circ}
-module

Let
R
be a linearly topologized ring and let
M
be a linearly topologized
R
-module. A formal series

\sum_{\alpha\in A}m_{\alpha}
of elements
m_{\alpha}\in M
, indexed by any set
A
, is said to be (an
A
-series)
unconditionally convergent to
m\in M
if, for any open submodule
N
of
M
, there is a finite subset
A_{N}
of
A

such that

\sum_{\alpha\in F}m_{\alpha}\in m+N
for any finite set
F
,
A_{N}\subset F\subset A
. Then
m_{\alpha}\in N
for any
\alpha\notin A_{N}
and we write
m=\sum_{\alpha\in A}m_{\alpha}
. If
M
is separated and complete and
\{m_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
is a family of elements of
M
such that, for any open
R
-submodule
P
of
M
,
m_{\alpha}\in P
, for almost all
\alpha\in A
, then

m=\sum_{\alpha\in A}m_{\alpha}
, for a unique
m\in M
.
In particular, if
M
is discrete, an
A
-series
\sum_{\alpha\in A}m_{\alpha}
of elements
m_{\alpha}\in M
is unconditionally convergent if and only if
m_{\alpha}=0
for all but a finite set of
\alpha\in A
. In that case the
A
-series converges to the finite sum
\sum^{\prime}_{\alpha\in A}m_{\alpha}
of non-zero
m_{\alpha}
’s.

For any set
X
,
{\mathfrak{P}}(X)
(resp.
{\mathscr{F}}(X)
) will be the family of (resp. finite) subsets of
X
. We use the term
clopen
to indicate a subset of a topological space
X
which is both open and closed: the set of clopens of
X
is denoted
\Sigma(X)
. We adopt the convention that “compact” or “locally compact” does not imply Hausdorff. The family of compact open subsets of a topological space
X
is denoted by
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
.

For any subset
U\subset X
, we denote by
\chi_{U}^{(X)}=\chi_{U}^{(X,k)}:X\to\{0,1\}\subset k
, or just
\chi_{U}
, the characteristic function of
U\subset X
. If
U=\{x\}
we also write
\chi_{x}^{(X)}
, or simply
\chi_{x}
, for
\chi_{\{x\}}^{(X)}
. If
X
is a topological space and
U\in\Sigma(X)
, then

\chi_{U}^{(X)}
is a continuous function
X\to k
.

We denote by
{{\mathcal{T}}}op
the category of topological spaces and continuous maps.

For generalities on quasi-abelian categories we refer to
[
23
]
. In a quasi-abelian category
{\mathscr{C}}
, a
strict subobject
(resp. a
strict quotient object
) of
X\in{\mathscr{C}}
is the kernel (resp. cokernel) of a
{\mathscr{C}}
-morphism
X\to Y
(resp.
Y\to X
). In the category
{{\mathcal{T}}}op
an
open
(resp.
closed
)
embedding

X\to Y
is a homeomorphism of
X
to an open (resp. closed) subspace
X^{\prime}
of
Y
. In the categories of complete topological modules considered in this paper a strict subobject is not in general a closed embedding of the underlying topological spaces. See Remark
5.10
below.

1
Introduction

The origin of the present discussion lies in a problem my Teacher Iacopo Barsotti had suggested
in view of my 1974 Padova Thesis. Namely, the function-theoretic interpretation of self-duality for

p
-divisible groups attached to an ordinary abelian variety in characteristic
p>0
.
In particular, that had to include a functional interpretation of some of the Barsotti-Witt rings of bivectors attached by Barsotti
to any
p
-divisible group. After partial results obtained at that time, the problem was left dormient for
over two score because of difficulties related to topological algebra, and in particular to duality, over a
p
-adic
ring and for lack of an immediate application.
More recently, I came back to this question : the motivation was to show that a certain entire

p
-adic analytic function I had encountered in my thesis
[
2
]
,
[
3
]
could be useful in the construction of an analytic Fourier theory for bounded uniformly continuous functions on
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
with
p
-adically entire test functions very similar to the Fourier expansion of a function
{\mathbb{R}}\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathbb{C}}
in terms of
\{\exp{{2i\pi z}\over n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
; this will appear in
[
4
]
. Moreover, we had realized over time that the weak dual of the Hopf
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-algebra of uniformy continuous functions
{\mathbb{G}}_{a}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
, namely the Hopf
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-algebra
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
of “uniform”
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-valued measures on
{\mathbb{G}}_{a}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, equipped with the topology of uniform convergence on balls of one same radius
is nothing but Fontaine’s ring
{\bf A}_{\inf}={\rm W}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})
on the perfectoid field
C
=
t
-adic completion of

{\mathbb{F}}_{p}((t^{1/p^{\infty}}))
, where
t^{1/p^{n}}=\delta_{1/p^{n}}-\delta_{0}
for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
{\rm W}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})
is equipped with the product topology.
Of course, the realization of a non trivial intersection with Fontaine’s theory
[
13
]
was a major motivation for completing this research.

Foundations of functional analysis over a very general linearly topologized ring is separately discussed in
[
5
]
, but we have summarized the essentials in
sections
2
and
3
so that, with the precious reference to
[
14
, Chap. 8]
, this paper stays self-contained.

We fix a separated and complete linearly topologized ring
k
satisfying properties
(
2.1
) below (
e.g.

k={\mathbb{F}}_{p}
or
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
, but not
k={\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, where
p
is a prime number).
We develop the basics of
k
-valued integration theory over locally compact paracompact
0
-dimensional topological (or uniform) spaces : we call them “
td
-spaces” as in
[
17
]
and
[
11
]
.
This category of spaces is sufficiently general to include non-archimedean locally analytic varieties (and in particular locally analytic groups)
of the type considered by Schneider and Teitelbaum
[
21
]
,
[
22
]
which arise from finite field extensions
L\supset K\supset{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, as the set
V={\mathbb{V}}(L)
(resp.
G={\mathbb{G}}(L)
) of
L
-valued points of an algebraic variety
{\mathbb{V}}
(resp. algebraic group
{\mathbb{G}}
) defined over
K
.
In that case, for any further completely valued extension
E\supset K
, our methods permit to study continuous (resp. uniformly continuous) functions on
V
and
G
with values
in
k=\widetilde{E}
or
k=E^{\circ}
, and the dual notions of measures on
V
and
G
which will be introduced below.
Notice that we do not consider explicitly here
E
-valued unbounded
functions and measures on
V
or
G
, nor do we discuss differentiability or local analyticity of functions and the corresponding spaces of distributions.
We content ourselves with an abstract duality theory for (resp. uniformly) continuous bounded functions and measures on (resp. uniform)
td
-spaces and
td
-groups.

We decided to revisit these topics because topological algebra over a linearly topologized ring presents remarkable difficulties, related to the description of limits and colimits, and in particular of kernels and cokernels.
Such categories of topological modules are in general not abelian : in some cases of interest we get however quasi-abelian categories
[
23
]
, namely exact categories with kernels and cokernels. We are especially interested in the case when those categories carry a closed monoidal structure compatible with the additive structure.
We deal with these problems systematically in
[
5
]
, and summarize in section
2
below the results which are relevant for our present purposes. Our approach to topological algebra borrows from the one of Gabber and Ramero
[
14
]
, and we refer to their very informative book project whenever helpful.

The difficulties arising in topological algebra recently led Clausen and Scholze to base their theory of analytic stacks on the more robust notion of
condensed sets
, namely sheaves on a Grothendieck site of profinite sets
[
25
]
. More recently still, they chose to restrict their foundational universe to
light
condensed sets so obtaining a more straightforward theory
[
26
]
. We were tempted to follow their approach, but we were facing two difficulties. On the one hand, the most interesting examples of integration pairing we had in mind involve
uniform

td
-spaces. Such notion however does not appear, as far as we understand, in the present state of art of Condensed Mathematics. For viewing uniform
td
-spaces as condensed sets, we might have adopted André’s notion of a
uniform sheaf
, nicely explained in
[
1
]
;
but that looked like a long term project.

On the other hand, part of the charm of Fourier theory lies in the possibility of exhibiting explicit dual topological bases for spaces of functions and of measures, which could hardly be done in a general framework of triangulated categories of
quasi-coherent sheaves. Certainly, in the setting of topological
k
-modules duality is not well-behaved in general : we did our best in some particular cases of perfect duality. For example, in section
3
we define weak and strong duals and establish the yoga for their application to limits, colimits, tensor products in the category
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
.
Those results seem to be new and are used all over the present paper.

Section
4
is completely topological. It provides a fairly complete description of
td
-spaces and of a certain type of uniform structures, called
td
-uniformities
, on them. As mentioned above, a
td
-space may naturally be seen as a
condensed set, and the restriction to light condensed sets would be the proper setting to discuss
metrizable

td
-spaces of which we give an
ad hoc
characterization. We dedicate some effort to describe the family of
td
-uniformities
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
(see section
4.4
below) on a given
td
-space
X
. For us
{\mathscr{J}}
is a cofiltered projective sub-system of the system
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
of all discrete quotient spaces
\pi_{D}:X\longrightarrow D
of
X
, where the quotient map
\pi_{D}
is proper. It turns out that in the category of topological spaces (resp. of uniform spaces) for any
td
-space
X
(resp. for any
td
-uniform space
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
)

(1.0.1)

X={\rm colim}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}C={\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}D\;\;\mbox{(resp.}\;(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})={\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}(D,\Theta_{D})\;\mbox{)}

(where the uniformity
\Theta_{D}
on the discrete space
D
is meant to be the discrete uniformity).

In section
5
we analyze the structure of the topological
k
-module
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
of continuous functions on
a
td
-space
X
equipped with the topology of uniform convergence on compact subsets of
X
. For a
td
-uniform space
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
we similarly analyze the space
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)

of uniformly continuous functions, with the topology of uniform convergence on
X
.
We also introduce the space
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)
, of functions with
almost compact support
(equivalently,
vanishing at infinity
), with the topology of uniform convergence on
X
. For any uniform structure
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
on
X
,

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)
is a closed subspace of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
, while it is a dense subset of
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
.
Our strategy for understanding any of the topological
k
-modules of continuous functions above, for a general
td
-space
X
, is based on a previous discussion of the simpler special cases of
X=C
a Stone space, and
X=D
a discrete space. We then use the description
1.0.1
to clarify the structure of the previous topological
k
-modules in the general case in terms of limits or colimits of simpler structures.

In the following section
6
we discuss
the dual structures of spaces of measures; their topology comes in the classical variants of “strong” and “weak”. We concentrate on the choices that will be more useful to us in the future. We use intensively the results of section
3
. Duality considerations appear both in
[
21
]
,
[
22
]
, and in
[
6
]
,
[
7
]
, but they are there finalized to the study of
p
-adic representations and appeal to sophisticated methods of
p
-adic functional analysis
over a field
.
We work instead on first principles over a linearly topologized ring
k
with topological
k
-modules of continuous functions and measures. Most of these results are quite subtle and, to the best of our knowledge, new.

Our conceptually naive approach based on the general description of certain subcategories of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}

[
5
]

and on some basic duality results, still permits to distinguish important patterns.
After the abstract definition in section
6
of dual spaces of measures for various spaces of continuous functions, section
7
proceeds with an independent and explicit description of
k
-valued measures on
X
as functions on measurable sets.

It turns out that the weak dual
{\mathscr{D}}(X,k)
(resp.
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)
) of the space
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)
(resp.
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
)
admits a natural direct description (see Proposition
7.2
).
Moreover, the weak dual of
{\mathscr{D}}(X,k)
(resp.
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)
) identifies with
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)
(resp.
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
) (
6.13.1
) (resp. (
6.11.1
)).
In partial contrast, if
X
is equipped with a
td
-uniform structure
\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}
, the space of
uniform measures on
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
is the weak dual
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
(
6.13.9
), while the latter is the
strong
dual of the former (
6.13.11
). The weak dual of
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
is instead the space
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
of continuous functions on
X
(
6.13.12
).

After the very general theory developed so far, section
8
is regrettably restricted to commutative
td
-groups and is still perhaps too short. We have summarized here without proofs some well-known constructions for commutative formal groups which will come out handy in the next sections.
We hope to be
able to come back to this topic in the future seriously enough to be able to illustrate some instance of Fourier theory on a classical non-compact non-commutative
td
-group. The
td
-groups studied by Wesolek
[
27
]
,
[
28
]
seem
to offer a rich area of application.

We then come to the example we had in mind, namely Fourier theory for bounded (uniformly) continuous
p
-adic functions on
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

In section
9.1
we present in a slightly unusual way the Mahler-Amice theory for
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-valued functions and measures on
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
.
We explain our view of the Iwasawa identification of the formal torus
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[[T]]
(where
{\mathbb{P}}(T)=T\widehat{\otimes}1+1\widehat{\otimes}T+T\widehat{\otimes}T
) with the Hopf algebra
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})={\mathscr{C}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{\prime}_{\rm weak}
of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-valued measures on
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
, via the map
T\longmapsto\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{0}
(see
\mathit{3}
of Proposition
9.2
below).
An observation which does not seem to appear explicitly in the literature is the fact
that the
(p,T)
-adic topology on
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[[T]]
corresponds to the topology
of uniform convergence on any family of balls
a+p^{h}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
of the same radius
p^{h}
on
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
. We name it the
natural topology
of
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
. The proof is actually not immediate (see Proposition
9.6
below).

In section
10.1
we extend the results of section
9.1
to the case of the Hopf algebra
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-valued uniform measures on
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. We show that the
p
-adic filtration makes
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
into a strict
p
-adic ring with residue ring
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{F}}_{p})\cong\widehat{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}[[t^{1/p^{\infty}}]]}
. Here
t=\delta_{1}-\delta_{0}
, where
\delta_{a}
(resp.
\Delta_{a}
) is the
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-valued (resp.
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-valued) Dirac mass concentrated in
a\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. So, for the
p
-adic topology

(1.0.2)

\widetilde{T}={\rm lim}_{n}(\Delta_{1/p^{n}}-\Delta_{0})^{p^{n}}=[\delta_{1}-\delta_{0}]\in{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})={\rm W}\left(\widehat{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}[[t^{1/p^{\infty}}]]}\right)\;.

The reader will then recognize the appearance of Fontaine’s ring
{\bf A}_{\inf}
in the guise of the ring of uniform
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-valued measures on
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
Again, it is more convenient to endow
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
with the (weaker)
natural topology
, namely the topology
of uniform convergence on balls of a given radius of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. Our main result here is the identification
of
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
equipped with the natural topology, with the completion of its subring
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[\widetilde{T}^{1/p^{\infty}}]
, for
\widetilde{T}
as in (
1.0.2
), in the
(p,\widetilde{T})
-adic topology (see
\mathit{4}
of Proposition
10.2
).
The reason why it is reasonable to endow
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
with the natural topology is twofold. First of all,
it is the topology used in the general theory of section
6
and it yields in particular the left-weak and right-strong perfect duality pairing (
6.14.1
)

(1.0.3)

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\times{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\longrightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\;\;,\;\;(\mu,f)\longmapsto\int_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}f(x)d\mu(x)\;,

in which the choice of topology plays a crucial role.
Secondly, the natural topology is sufficiently weak to allow the interesting changes of variable of section
10.2
. In particular the
canonical measure

\mu_{\rm can}\in{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})={\bf A}_{\inf}
(see Proposition
10.7
) is related to
T
via
an analytic procedure, based on the Artin-Hasse exponential, which would not converge
p
-adically. The canonical measure will play a central role in the analytic Fourier theory of
[
4
]
.

In section
11
we develop the “uniformly continuous” Fourier theory for the pairing (
11.3
). This is a novelty of this paper. For
q\in S:={\mathbb{Z}}[1/p]\cap{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
we define a uniformly continuous function
{x\choose q}^{(p)}:{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
(see
(
11.0.1
)). We prove orthogonality (see (
11.4.1
)) of
\{{x\choose q}^{(p)}\}_{q\in S}
and
\{\widetilde{T}^{q}\}_{q\in S}

w.r.t. the pairing (
11.3
). In Proposition
11.6.1
, we compute the Fourier expansion of the additive character (see (
11.5.7
))

(1.0.4)

{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\longrightarrow 1+{\mathbb{C}}_{p}^{\circ\circ}\;\;,\;\;s\longmapsto(\gamma^{s})^{\sharp}=\sum_{q\in S}{s\choose q}^{(p)}(\varpi^{q})^{\sharp}\;,

where
\gamma=1+\varpi\in 1+{\mathbb{C}}_{p}^{\flat\circ\circ}
, where we have used the notation of
[
24
]
.
In the paper
[
4
]
the present uniformly continuous Fourier theory for
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
will be improved into a
p
-adically entire Fourier theory in which the dual basis of
\{\mu_{\rm can}^{q}\}_{q\in S}

will be a family
\{G_{q}(x)\}_{q\in S}
of
p
-adically entire functions, obtained from the entire function
\Psi
of
[
3
]
, such that
(G_{q})_{|{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
for any
q\in S
. In
loc.cit.
formula (
1.0.4
) will be ameliorated into the analytic formula

(1.0.5)

(\gamma^{x})^{\sharp}=\sum_{q\in S}G_{q}(x)\int_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}(\gamma^{s})^{\sharp}\,d\mu_{\rm can}^{q}(s)

which has applications to the theory of
p
-adic Mellin transforms. The existence and properties of
\mu_{\rm can}\in{\bf A}_{\inf}
have gone unnoticed till now.

1.1
Aknowledgements

The main ideas of this paper had already appeared in my thesis, and were largely inspired by my Teacher Iacopo Barsotti. I wish to express my gratitude to Maurizio Cailotto who patiently listened to my ideas and often corrected them.
I profited from discussions with Umberto Marconi who helped me to understand the topology of locally compact
0
-dimensional spaces and the uniformities on them.

Finally, I want to express my gratitude to Adrian Iovita for his constant encouragement.

2
Generalities on linear topologies

2.1
Linear topologies

Let
k
be a separated and complete linearly topologized ring. We require that
k
satisfies the following properties

•

(
\omega
-admissibility)
k
has a countable basis of open ideals;

•

(the “finiteness” condition) any open ideal of
k
contains a finitely generated open ideal;

•

(the “op” condition)
for any open ideals
I,J
of
k
, the ideal
IJ
is open in
k
.

We will also denote by
{\mathcal{P}}(k)
the family of open ideals of
k
.

Remark 2.1
.

By
[
14
, Rmk. 8.3.3 (iii)]
for any linearly topologized ring
k
(satisfying
\omega
-admissibility and finiteness) the op condition is equivalent to the following condition

•

There exists a basis
{\mathscr{B}}
of open ideals of
k
such that, for any
I\in{\mathscr{B}}
, the ideal
I^{2}
is open.

We will work in the category
{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm u}_{k}
of linearly topologized
k
-modules
M
such that
the map multiplication by scalars

k\times M\longrightarrow M\;\;,\;\;(r,m)\longmapsto rm

is
uniformly continuous
for the product uniformity of
k\times M
. This condition means that the topology of
M
is weaker than the
naive canonical topology
of
M
, the latter being the topology with fundamental system of open submodules the family
\{IM\}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}
. The same condition is also equivalent to the fact that the
k
-linearly topologized module
M
be bounded
[
5
]
. Morphisms of
{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm u}_{k}
are continuous
k
-linear maps. We are interested in the full subcategory
{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm u}_{k}
(resp.
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
) of separated (resp. separated and complete) modules.
The category
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
is the one of
[
14
, §15.1.2]
(implicitly described in
[
15
, Chap. 0, §7.7.1]
). Its full subcategory of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
consisting of objects with a countable basis of open
k
-submodules is denoted by
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
. In
[
5
]
we prove that

{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
is a quasi-abelian category with enough injectives
[
23
, Defn. 1.3.18]
.
For any object
M
of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
,
{\mathcal{P}}(M)
will denote the family of open
k
-submodules of
M
. Notice that if the topology of the object
M
of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
is discrete, then
IM=(0)
for some
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
.
For any
k/I
-module
M
we will denote by
M^{\rm dis\,}
the object of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
with underlying
k^{\rm for}
-module
M^{\rm for}
, equipped with the discrete topology.

The category
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
admits all limits and colimits.
The former are calculated in the category of
k
-modules and carry the weak topology of the canonical projections, but the latter need some explanation (to be given below). So, a limit will be denoted by
{\rm lim}
while a colimit will carry an apex
(-)^{\rm u}
as in
{\rm colim}^{\rm u}
.
For an inductive system
\{M_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, let

(M:={\rm colim}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha},\{\iota_{\alpha}:M_{\alpha}\to M\}_{\alpha})

be the familiar colimit in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}
.
Then

{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}
is the (separated) completion of
M
equipped with the
k
-linear topology for which a basis of open
k
-submodules consists of the submodules
N\subset M
such that
\iota_{\alpha}^{-1}(N)\in{\mathcal{P}}(M_{\alpha})
for any
\alpha
, and
such that there exists
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
with
IM\subset N
.
If
k
and all
M_{\alpha}
’s are discrete, then

{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}
is discrete, as well.

The cokernel of a morphism
f:N\to M
in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
is

{\rm Coker}^{\rm u}(f)={\rm lim}_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}(M)}M/(f(N)+P)

where
M/(f(N)+P)
is calculated in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}
and is endowed with the discrete topology. In general,
{\rm Coker}^{\rm u}(f)
contains as a subspace
M/\overline{f(N)}
, which is the cokernel of
f
in
{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm u}_{k}
.
If
M\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
, then
{\rm Coker}^{\rm u}(f)=M/\overline{f(N)}
; in particular, if
M\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
and
f
is closed, then

({\rm Coker}^{\rm u}(f))^{\rm for}
coincides with
{\rm Coker}(f^{\rm for})
.
For any injective
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
-morphism
i:N\hookrightarrow M
, we write
(M/N)^{\rm u}
for
{\rm Coker}^{\rm u}(i)
.
For an inductive system
\{M_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
,

(2.1.1)

{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}(M_{\alpha}/IM_{\alpha})^{\rm u}\;.

An object
M
of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
whose topology is the naive canonical topology is called
canonical
, or
k
-
canonical
if necessary. The full subcategory of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
consisting of
k
-canonical modules is a quasi-abelian category with enough projectives
[
23
, Defn. 1.3.18]
, denoted
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
.
See
[
5
]
for more detail on canonical modules, and in particular for the fact that the fully faithful functor
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\subset{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
admits a right adjoint
(-)^{\rm can}
, where, for
M\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
,
M^{\rm can}
is simply
M
endowed with the naive canonical topology.

Remark 2.2
.

The crucial consequence of our assumptions on
k
is that any
M\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
is complete in its naive canonical topology. This is proven in
[
5
]
. A weaker result states that this is the case if
k
is a
I
-adic ring for a finitely generated ideal
I
. This appears as Lemma 8.3.12 (b) of
[
14
]
. In the special case when
M\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
, this also appears
as Proposition 0.7.2.5 of
[
16
]
.

Remark 2.3
.

For any
M\in{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}
, let
\wideparen{M}
be the completion of
M
equipped with
the naive canonical topology. It follows from
[
14
, Rmk. 8.3.3]
that the topology of the
k
-module
\wideparen{M}
is again the
naive canonical topology.

Remark 2.4
.

Assume in (
2.1.1
)
M_{\alpha}\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
,
\forall\alpha\in A
, and let
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
be finitely generated. Then, by

[
14
, Rmk. 8.3.3 (iv)]
,

\overline{IM_{\alpha}}=IM_{\alpha}
,
\forall\alpha\in A
. Therefore in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}
, for
M={\rm colim}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
we have

M/IM={\rm colim}_{\alpha\in A}(M_{\alpha}/IM_{\alpha})\;,

so that
(
2.1.1
) becomes

(2.4.1)

{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}(M/IM)^{\rm dis\,}=\wideparen{M}\;.

It follows from Remark
2.3
that
\wideparen{M}
is canonical, hence that

{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
is closed under
{\rm colim}^{\rm u}
(
i.e.
under colimits in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
).

On the other hand, since
(-)^{\rm can}
is a right adjoint,
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
admits projective limits, calculated by applying
(-)^{\rm can}
to
{\rm lim}
: the latter are denoted by
{\rm lim}^{\rm can}
and will be described below.

2.2
Sums and products

For any family
M_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in A
, of objects of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, the direct sum and direct product exist and will be denoted by

\mathop{{\bigoplus}^{\rm u}}\limits_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}\;\;,\;\;\prod_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}\;,

respectively.
We explicitly notice that
\mathop{{\bigoplus}^{\rm u}}\limits_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
, called the
uniform direct sum
of the
M_{\alpha}
’s, is the completion of the algebraic direct sum

\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
of the algebraic
k
-modules
M_{\alpha}
’s, equipped with the
k
-linear topology for which a fundamental system of open
k
-modules consists of the
k
-submodules

\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}(U_{\alpha}+IM_{\alpha})\;\;\mbox{such that}\;U_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{P}}(M_{\alpha})\;\;\forall\alpha\;,\;\mbox{and}\;I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)\;\mbox{is independent of}\,\alpha\;.

Then the
k
-module underlying
\mathop{{\bigoplus}^{\rm u}}\limits_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
differs in general from the algebraic direct sum
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
, even if all
M_{\alpha}
are discrete.
But if
k
is discrete as well, then

\mathop{{\bigoplus}^{\rm u}}\limits_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
coincides with
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
equipped with the discrete topology.

For
M_{\alpha}
a copy of
M\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
for any
\alpha\in A
, we denote by
M^{(A,{\rm u})}
the uniform direct sum of the
M_{\alpha}
’s.
When all
M_{\alpha}
’s are canonical,
{\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
is the completion of
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
in its naive canonical topology.
In particular, for any small set
A
, we have defined

k^{(A,{\rm u})}
. The following result is proven in
[
5
]
.

Theorem 2.5
.

{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
is the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
consisting of
quotients of objects of the form
k^{(A,{\rm u})}
, for small sets
A
.

Remark 2.6
.

It follows from Theorem
2.5
that an object of
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
is the
cokernel of a morphism of the form
k^{(A,{\rm u})}\to k^{(B,{\rm u})}
for small sets
A,B
.

For any family
M_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in A
, of objects of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, as before, it will be useful to introduce the
uniform box product
of the
M_{\alpha}
’s :

(2.6.1)

{\prod}_{\alpha\in A}^{\square,{\rm u}}M_{\alpha}\;.

Set-theoretically, (
2.6.1
) coincides with
\prod_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
, but a basis of open submodules of (
2.6.1
) consists
of all
\prod_{\alpha\in A}U_{\alpha}
, with
U_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{P}}(M_{\alpha})
, for which there exists
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
such that

IM_{\alpha}\subset U_{\alpha}
, for any
\alpha\in A
.
Notice that

(2.6.2)

{\prod}_{\alpha\in A}^{\square,{\rm u}}M_{\alpha}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\prod}_{\alpha\in A}^{\square,{\rm u}}(M_{\alpha}/IM_{\alpha})^{\rm u}\;.

There are natural continuous injections

(2.6.3)

{\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}\subset{\prod}_{\alpha\in A}^{\square,u}M_{\alpha}\subset{\prod}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}\;,

the first of which is a closed subspace embedding while the second is a bijection. On the other hand, the inclusion

{\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}\subset{\prod}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}

is dense.
More generally, for a projective system
(M_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
, indexed by the cofiltered poset
A
, in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, the
k
-submodule

{{\rm lim}_{\alpha\in A}}M_{\alpha}^{\rm for}
of
\prod_{\alpha\in A}^{\square,u}M_{\alpha}
is closed. We then define the

uniform box limit
of the previous projective system

(2.6.4)

{{\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha\in A}}M_{\alpha}

as this closed submodule equipped with the subspace topology. In particular,

(2.6.5)

({{\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha\in A}}M_{\alpha})^{\rm for}={{\rm lim}_{\alpha\in A}}M_{\alpha}^{\rm for}\;.

From (
2.6.2
) we deduce that

(2.6.6)

{{\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha\in A}}M_{\alpha}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{{\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha\in A}}(M_{\alpha}/IM_{\alpha})^{\rm u}\;.

Corollary 2.7
.

In the particular case when every
M_{\alpha}
is
k
-canonical, we have

(2.7.1)

\begin{split}{\prod}_{\alpha\in A}^{\square,{\rm u}}M_{\alpha}&={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}(\prod_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}/{IM_{\alpha}})^{\rm dis\,}\\
\mbox{(resp.}\;{{\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha\in A}}M_{\alpha}&={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}({\rm lim}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}/{IM_{\alpha}})^{\rm dis\,}\;\;\mbox{)}\;.\end{split}

So, in this special case,
\prod^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
(risp.
{{\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha\in A}}M_{\alpha}
) coincides with
\prod_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}^{\rm for}
(risp.
{{\rm lim}_{\alpha\in A}}M_{\alpha}^{\rm for}
)
equipped with the naive canonical topology; it then coincides with the
direct product
{\prod}^{{\rm can}}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
(risp. the limit
{{\rm lim}^{\rm can}_{\alpha\in A}}M_{\alpha}
) in the category
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
. If, for any
\alpha\in A
,
M_{\alpha}=M\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
, then
\prod^{\rm can}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
is denoted
M^{A,{\rm can}}
and it is simply the product
k
-module
M^{A}
equipped with the naive
k
-canonical topology. In particular, for any
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
we have

(2.7.2)

\begin{split}{\prod}_{\alpha\in A}^{\square,{\rm u}}M_{\alpha}/I{\prod}_{\alpha\in A}^{\square,{\rm u}}M_{\alpha}&=(\prod_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}/{IM_{\alpha}})^{\rm dis\,}\\
\mbox{(resp.}\;{{\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha\in A}}M_{\alpha}/I{{\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha\in A}}M_{\alpha}&=({\rm lim}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}/{IM_{\alpha}})^{\rm dis\,}\;\;\mbox{)}\;.\end{split}

2.3
Tensor product

For two objects
M
and
N
of
{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, we define
M\otimes^{\rm u}_{k}N\in{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm u}_{k}
as
M\otimes_{k}N
equipped with the topology with basis of open
k
-submodules
\{P\otimes_{k}N+M\otimes_{k}Q\}
for
P\in{\mathcal{P}}(M)
and
Q\in{\mathcal{P}}(N)
.

Remark 2.8
.

For
M,N,X\in{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, let
{\rm Bil}^{\rm u}_{k}(M\times N,X)
denote the
k
-module of
k
-bilinear functions
M\times N\longrightarrow X
which are uniformly continuous for the product uniformity of
M\times N
. Then

\otimes^{\rm u}_{k}:M\times N\longrightarrow M\otimes^{\rm u}_{k}N
belongs to
{\rm Bil}^{\rm u}_{k}(M\times N,M\otimes^{\rm u}_{k}N)
and
the pair
(M\otimes^{\rm u}_{k}N,\otimes^{\rm u}_{k})
corepresents the functor

(2.8.1)

\begin{split}{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm u}_{k}&\longrightarrow{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}\\
X&\longmapsto{\rm Bil}^{\rm u}_{k}(M\times N,X)\;.\end{split}

For two objects
M
and
N
of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, we set

(2.8.2)

M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N=\widehat{M\otimes^{\rm u}_{k}N}={\rm lim}^{\rm u}_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}(M),Q\in{\mathcal{P}}(N)}(M/P\otimes_{k}N/Q)\;.

The category
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, equipped with the tensor product
\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}
, which coincides with the one
of
[
15
,
\bf 0
.7.7]
(see also
[
10
, Chap. III, §2, Exer. 28]
), is a symmetric monoidal category.
For
M\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
, the functor

{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}\;\;,\;\;X\longmapsto M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}X\;,

restricts to a functor

(2.8.3)

\begin{split}M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}-:{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}&\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}\\
X&\longmapsto M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}X\;.\end{split}

It follows from
[
14
, Lemma 15.1.27 (i)]
that the functor (
2.8.3
) is
strongly right exact
in the sense that it preserves cokernels of arbitrary morphisms in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
and
left exact
in the sense that it preserves kernels of strict morphisms in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}

[
23
, Defn. 1.1.12]
. In other words, the functor (
2.8.3
) preserves cokernels and images in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
.

Remark 2.9
.

For
M,N\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
,

\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}:M\times N\longrightarrow M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N
belongs to
{\rm Bil}^{\rm u}_{k}(M\times N,M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N)
and
the pair
(M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N,\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k})
corepresents the restriction to
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
of the
functor (
2.8.1
).

Corollary 2.10
.

If
M
,
N
are canonical
k
-modules then

M\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}N

is canonical.

Proof.

Let
\phi:k^{(A,{\rm u})}\to M
and
\psi:k^{(B,{\rm u})}\to N
be strict epimorphisms. Then

k^{(A,{\rm u})}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}k^{(B,{\rm u})}=k^{(A\times B,{\rm u})}
, and the morphism
\phi\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}\psi:k^{(A\times B,{\rm u})}\to M\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}N
is a strict epimorphism, as well. In fact,
\phi\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}\psi
decomposes into the product

k^{(A,{\rm u})}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}k^{(B,{\rm u})}\xrightarrow{\ {\rm id}_{k^{(A,{\rm u})}}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}\psi\ }k^{(A,{\rm u})}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N\xrightarrow{\ \phi\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}{\rm id}_{N}\ }M\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}N

The functor
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
,
X\longmapsto k^{(A,{\rm u})}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}X
(resp.
Y\longmapsto Y\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N
) is right exact, so that both
{\rm id}_{k^{(A,{\rm u})}}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}\psi
and
\phi\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}{\rm id}_{N}
are strict epimorphisms, and therefore so is their composition
\phi\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}\psi

[
23
, §1.1.3]
.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

For
M,N
canonical,
M\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}N=M\otimes^{\rm u}_{k}N
is simply
M\otimes_{k}N
equipped with the naive canonical topology.

Definition 2.12
.

An object
P\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
is
pro-flat
if, for any
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
,
(P/IP)^{\rm u}
is flat in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k/I}
.

According to
[
14
, Lemma 15.1.27 (ii)]
, if
P\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
is pro-flat, the functor

P\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}-:{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}

is left exact in the above sense
i.e.
it preserves kernels of strict morphisms.

Remark 2.13
.

The functor
(-)^{\rm can}:{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}\longrightarrow{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
does not commute in general with
\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}
.

Lemma 2.14
.

1.

Let
\{M_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
(resp.
\{N_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in B}
) be an inductive system in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
and let

M={\rm colim}_{\alpha\in A}^{\rm u}M_{\alpha}\;\;,\;\;N={\rm colim}_{\beta\in B}^{\rm u}N_{\beta}

in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
. Then we have a natural morphism

(2.14.1)

{\rm colim}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\rm u}(M_{\alpha}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N_{\beta})\xrightarrow{\ \ }M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N\;.

2.

Let
\{M_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
(resp.
\{N_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in B}
) be a projective system in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
and let

M={\rm lim}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}\;\;,\;\;N={\rm lim}_{\beta\in B}N_{\beta}

in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
. Then we have a natural morphism

(2.14.2)

M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\rm lim}_{\alpha,\beta}(M_{\alpha}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N_{\beta})\;.

Proof.

Clear.
∎

Proposition 2.15
.

\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}
commutes with colimits in
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
.

Proof.

Let
\{M_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
(resp.
\{N_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in B}
) be an inductive system in
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
and let

M={\rm colim}_{\alpha\in A}^{\rm u}M_{\alpha}\;\;,\;\;N={\rm colim}_{\beta\in B}^{\rm u}N_{\beta}

in
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
.
From Remark
2.4
we know that

M/IM={\rm colim}_{\alpha\in A}(M_{\alpha}/IM_{\alpha})\;\;,\;\;N/IN={\rm colim}_{\beta\in B}(N_{\beta}/IN_{\beta})\;.

Now, for any
X\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
,

X={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}X/IX\;.

Then we have

M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}(M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N)/I(M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}(M\otimes_{k}N)/I(M\otimes_{k}N)=

{\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}(M/IM\otimes_{k/I}N/IN)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}({\rm colim}_{\alpha\in A}(M_{\alpha}/IM_{\alpha})\otimes_{k/I}{\rm colim}_{\beta\in B}(N_{\beta}/IN_{\beta}))=

{\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\rm colim}_{\alpha\in A,\beta\in B}(M_{\alpha}/IM_{\alpha}\otimes_{k/I}N_{\beta}/IN_{\beta})={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\rm colim}_{\alpha\in A,\beta\in B}(M_{\alpha}\otimes_{k}N_{\beta})/I(M_{\alpha}\otimes_{k}N_{\beta})=

{\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\rm colim}_{\alpha\in A,\beta\in B}(M_{\alpha}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N_{\beta})/I(M_{\alpha}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N_{\beta})={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A,\beta\in B}M_{\alpha}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N_{\beta}\;.

∎

3
Duality

3.1
Weak and strong duals

We now define two internal
{\rm Hom}
functors in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
. Their difference depends on the fact that
for two objects
M,N
of
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}
, there are two natural topologies on the
k
-module
{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N)={\rm lim}_{F}{\rm Hom}_{k}(F,N)
, where
F
runs over the finitely generated submodules of
M
. Namely,
{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N)
may be equipped with the discrete topology or with the weak topology of the natural projections
{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N)\to{\rm Hom}_{k}(F,N)
where, for
F
as before,
{\rm Hom}_{k}(F,N)
is given the discrete topology. We denote by
{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N)^{\rm dis\,}
(resp.
{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N)^{\rm pfg}
, where “pfg” stands for “pro-finitely generated”) the corresponding objects of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
.
We first observe that there is a canonical isomorphism in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}

(3.0.1)

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M,N)={\rm lim}_{Q\in{\mathcal{P}}(N)}{\rm colim}_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}(M)}{\rm Hom}_{k}(M/P,N/Q)\;.

We set the following

Definition 3.1
.

For any objects
M,N
of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, we set

(3.1.1)

{\rm Hom}_{\rm weak}(M,N)={\rm lim}_{Q\in{\mathcal{P}}(N)}{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}(M)}{\rm Hom}_{k}(M/P,N/Q)^{\rm pfg}

(3.1.2)

{\rm Hom}_{\rm strong}(M,N)={\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{Q\in{\mathcal{P}}(N)}{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}(M)}{\rm Hom}_{k}(M/P,N/Q)^{\rm dis\,}

In particular, we set
M^{\prime}_{\rm strong}:={\rm Hom}_{\rm strong}(M,k)
(resp.
M^{\prime}_{\rm weak}:={\rm Hom}_{\rm weak}(M,k)
) and call it the
strong
(resp.
weak
) dual of
M
.

Proposition 3.2
.

For any
M,N\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, there is a natural
k
-linear isomorphism

(3.2.1)

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M,N)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }{\rm Hom}_{\rm strong}(M,N)^{\rm for}\;.

Proof.

1.

In formula (
3.1.2
) which defines

{\rm Hom}_{\rm strong}(M,N)
, the topological
k
-module

C(Q):={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}(M)}{\rm Hom}_{k}(M/P,N/Q)^{\rm dis\,}

is a discrete
k/I
-module for any
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
such that
IN\subset Q

and therefore identifies
k
-linearly with
the colimit

{\rm colim}_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}(M)}{\rm Hom}_{k}(M/P,N/Q)\;,

taken in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}
.

2.

Then

{\rm lim}_{Q\in{\mathcal{P}}(N)}C(Q)\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}

identifies
k
-linearly with the r.h.s. of (
3.0.1
). That is, there is a canonical
k
-linear isomorphism

(3.2.2)

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M,N)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }{\rm Hom}_{\rm strong}(M,N)^{\rm for}\;.

∎

Remark 3.3
.

1.

For any
M,N\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, the topology induced on
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M,N)
by the isomorphism (
3.2.1
) is usually called the

strong topology
of
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M,N)
. We conclude that
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M,N)
is separated and complete in its strong topology. The strong topology of
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M,N)
coincides with the topology of uniform convergence on
M
.

2.

We have a natural injective morphism with dense image

{\rm Hom}_{\rm strong}(M,N)\longrightarrow{\rm Hom}_{\rm weak}(M,N)

in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
.
The topology induced on
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M,N)
by the injective map

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M,N)\hookrightarrow{\rm Hom}_{\rm weak}(M,N)^{\rm for}

is called the

weak topology
of
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M,N)
.
Then
{\rm Hom}_{\rm weak}(M,N)
is the completion of

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M,N)
for its weak topology. The weak topology of
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M,N)
coincides with the topology of simple convergence on
M
.

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
M\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
. Then

(3.4.1)

M^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}(M/IM)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}

and

(3.4.2)

M^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}(M/IM)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}

Proof.

The first assertion holds because

M^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\rm Hom}_{k}(M/IM,k/I)^{\rm dis\,}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}(M/IM)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}\;.

For the second it follows from the definition (
3.1.1
) that

M^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\rm Hom}_{k}(M/IM,k/I)^{\rm pfg}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}(M/IM)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}\;.

∎

Definition 3.5
.

For a morphism
\varphi:M\longrightarrow N
in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, we get
transposed morphisms

\varphi^{\bf t}:N^{\prime}_{\rm strong}\longrightarrow M^{\prime}_{\rm strong}
and
\varphi^{\bf t}:N^{\prime}_{\rm weak}\longrightarrow M^{\prime}_{\rm weak}
.

3.2
Duals of limits and colimits

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
(M_{\alpha},j_{\alpha,\beta})_{\alpha\leq\beta}
(resp.
(M_{\alpha},\pi_{\alpha,\beta})_{\alpha\leq\beta}
) be an inductive
(resp. projective)
system in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, indexed by the filtered
poset
(A,\leq)
, and let
N\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
.
Then we have in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}

(3.6.1)

{\rm Hom}_{\rm weak}({\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha},N)={\rm lim}_{\alpha}{\rm Hom}_{\rm weak}(M_{\alpha},N)\;.

(3.6.2)

{\rm Hom}_{\rm strong}({\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha},N)={\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha}{\rm Hom}_{\rm strong}(M_{\alpha},N)\;;

(3.6.3)

{\rm Hom}_{?}({\rm lim}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha},N)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha}{\rm Hom}_{?}(M_{\alpha},N)

where
?={\rm weak},{\rm strong}
.
If
M_{\alpha}\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
for all
\alpha
and
N\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
, as well, we also have

(3.6.4)

{\rm Hom}_{?}({\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha},N)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha}{\rm Hom}_{?}(M_{\alpha},N)\;,

for
?={\rm weak},{\rm strong}
.

Proof.

Formula (
3.6.1
) is obtained by completion of the
tautological
k
-linear isomorphism

(3.6.5)

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}({\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha},N)={\rm lim}_{\alpha}\,{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M_{\alpha},N)

for the weak topology of the
l.h.s.
and the limit topology of the weak topologies of each term of the
r.h.s.
. Let

j_{\alpha}:M_{\alpha}\to{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}
denote the canonical morphism, for any
\alpha\in A
.
The topology of simple convergence on

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}({\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha},N)

has a fundamental system of open submodules

U(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};P_{\alpha_{1}},\dots,P_{\alpha_{n}};Q)\;,

consisting of the
k
-linear functions
f:{\rm colim}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}\to N
such that
f(j_{\alpha_{i}}(P_{\alpha_{i}}))\subset Q
, for
i=1,2,\dots,n
, where
P_{\alpha_{i}}
is a finitely generated
k
-submodule of
M_{\alpha_{i}}
and
Q
an open
k
-submodule of
N
.
It is clear that this topology coincides with the weak topology of the projections

{\rm lim}_{\alpha}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M_{\alpha},N)\to{\rm Hom}_{\rm weak}(M_{\alpha},N)
, for
\alpha\in A
.

To prove (
3.6.2
) we refer to the equality (
3.6.5
) and use the definition (
3.1.2
). We pick
Q\in{\mathcal{P}}(N)
.
Then

\begin{split}{\rm Hom}_{\rm strong}({\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha},N/Q)&={\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}({\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha})}\,{\rm Hom}_{k}(({\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha})/P,N/Q)^{\rm dis\,}=\\
{\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\beta\in B,P_{\beta}\in{\mathcal{P}}(M_{\beta})}&\,{\rm Hom}_{k}(({\rm colim}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha})/\pi_{\beta}^{-1}(P_{\beta}),N/Q)^{\rm dis\,}=\\
{\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha\in A,P_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{P}}(M_{\alpha})}&\,{\rm Hom}_{k}(M_{\alpha}/P_{\alpha},N/Q)^{\rm dis\,}={\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha}\,{\rm Hom}_{\rm strong}(M_{\alpha},N/Q)\;.\end{split}

Now, for (
3.6.3
) and
?={\rm weak},{\rm strong}
, we pick
Q\in{\mathcal{P}}(N)
. Let
\pi_{\alpha}:{\rm lim}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}\to M_{\alpha}
denote the canonical morphism, for any
\alpha\in A
. We set
!={\rm pfg}
if
?={\rm weak}
and
!={\rm dis\,}
if
?={\rm strong}
, and compute

(3.6.6)

\begin{split}{\rm Hom}_{?}({\rm lim}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha},N/Q)&={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}({\rm lim}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha})}{\rm Hom}_{k}(({\rm lim}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha})/P,N/Q)^{!}=\\
{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\beta\in B,P_{\beta}\in{\mathcal{P}}(M_{\beta})}&{\rm Hom}_{k}(({\rm lim}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha})/\pi_{\beta}^{-1}(P_{\beta}),N/Q)^{!}=\\
{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A,P_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{P}}(M_{\alpha})}&{\rm Hom}_{k}(M_{\alpha}/P_{\alpha},N/Q)^{!}\;,\end{split}

where in the last term the cofiltered projective system
\overline{\pi}_{\alpha,\beta}:M_{\beta}/P_{\beta}\rightarrow M_{\alpha}/P_{\alpha}
is induced by

\pi_{\alpha,\beta}:M_{\beta}\to M_{\alpha}
and
P_{\beta}\subset\pi_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}(P_{\alpha})
if
\beta\geq\alpha
, but
P_{\beta}=\pi_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}(P_{\alpha})
, for
\beta>>\alpha
. For two such systems
P=(\overline{\pi}_{\alpha,\beta})
,
\overline{\pi}_{\alpha,\beta}:M_{\beta}/P_{\beta}\rightarrow M_{\alpha}/P_{\alpha}
and

P^{\prime}=(\overline{\pi}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta})
,
\overline{\pi}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}:M_{\beta}/P^{\prime}_{\beta}\rightarrow M_{\alpha}/P^{\prime}_{\alpha}
such that
P^{\prime}_{\alpha}\subset P_{\alpha}
, for any
\alpha\in A
, we have a morphism

\varphi_{P,P^{\prime}}=(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\alpha}:(\overline{\pi}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta})\to(\overline{\pi}_{\alpha,\beta})
,
i.e.
a family of commutative diagrams

(3.6.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\beta}/P^{\prime}_{\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\beta}/P_{\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\alpha}/P^{\prime}_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\alpha}/P_{\alpha}\;\;.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi}_{\alpha,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\alpha}}

For a further system
P^{\prime\prime}=(\overline{\pi}^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha,\beta})
and a morphism
\varphi_{P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime}}:P^{\prime\prime}\to P^{\prime}
, we have
\varphi_{P,P^{\prime}}\circ\varphi_{P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime}}=\varphi_{P,P^{\prime\prime}}
. The inductive system in (
3.6.6
) then splits into

{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{(P,\varphi_{P,P^{\prime}})}{\rm Hom}_{k}(M_{\alpha}/P_{\alpha},N/Q)^{!}={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha}{\rm Hom}_{?}(M_{\alpha},N/Q)\;.

The proof of (
3.6.4
) follows the scheme of (
3.6.6
). Using (
2.7.2
) we obtain

(3.6.8)

\begin{split}{\rm Hom}_{?}({\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{\alpha}&M_{\alpha},N)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\rm Hom}_{k}({\rm lim}_{\alpha}(M_{\alpha}/IM_{\alpha}),N/IN)^{!}=\\
{\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\rm colim}_{\alpha\in A}&{\rm Hom}_{k}(M_{\alpha}/IM_{\alpha},N/IN)^{!}={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}{\rm Hom}_{?}(M_{\alpha},N)\;.\end{split}

∎

Corollary 3.7
.

For any family
(M_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}

we have

({\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha})^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\prod}_{\alpha\in A}(M_{\alpha})^{\prime}_{\rm weak}\;\;,\;\;({\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha})^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\prod}^{\square,\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}(M_{\alpha})^{\prime}_{\rm strong}\;,

({\prod}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha})_{?}^{\prime}={\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}(M_{\alpha})_{?}^{\prime}\;\;,\;\;\mbox{for $?={\rm weak},{\rm strong}$}\;.

If, for any
\alpha\in A
,
M_{\alpha}\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
, we also have

\left({\prod}^{\square,\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}\right)^{\prime}_{?}={\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}(M_{\alpha})_{?}^{\prime}\;\;,\;\;\mbox{for $?={\rm weak},{\rm strong}$}\;.

Remark 3.8
.

In
[
5
]
we define an internal
{\rm Hom}
, called
{\rm Hom}_{\rm strong}^{\rm can}
in
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
,
by simply setting
{\rm Hom}_{\rm strong}^{\rm can}(M,N)={\rm Hom}_{\rm strong}(M,N)^{\rm can}
, for any
M,N\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
. We prove that
({{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k},\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k},{\rm Hom}^{\rm can}_{\rm strong},k)

is a quasi-abelian closed symmetric monoidal category with all limits and colimits.
1
1

1

Moreover,
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
has enough projectives in the sense of
[
23
, §1.3.4]
and for every projective
P
, the functor

{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\longrightarrow{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;\;,\;\;X\longmapsto P\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}X

is left-exact (and therefore exact) in the sense of
[
23
, Defn. 1.1.12]
.

So, for
M\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
the dual in
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
, is
(M^{\prime}_{\rm strong})^{\rm can}
hence differs in general from
M^{\prime}_{\rm strong}
.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
M\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
and let

M={\rm Coker}^{\rm u}(k^{(A,{\rm u})}\xrightarrow{\ \varphi\ }k^{(B,{\rm u})})

be a presentation of
M
as in Remark
2.6
. Then

(3.9.1)

M^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\rm Ker}(k^{B,{\rm can}}\xrightarrow{\ \varphi^{\bf t}\ }k^{A,{\rm can}})\;.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.4
, we may assume that
k
and
M
are discrete so that

M={\rm Coker}(k^{(A)}\xrightarrow{\ \varphi\ }k^{(B)})

in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}
. The functor
(-)^{\bf t}={\rm Hom}_{k}(-,k)
is then left exact and

{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,k)={\rm Ker}(k^{B}\xrightarrow{\ \varphi^{\bf t}\ }k^{A})\;.

Going back to the situation of the statement,

M^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\rm Ker}(k^{B}/Ik^{B}\xrightarrow{\ \varphi^{\bf t}\mod I\ }k^{A}/Ik^{A})

which coincides with the r.h.s. of (
3.9.1
).
∎

Definition 3.10
.

Let
M
,
N
be objects of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
and let

(3.10.1)

\begin{split}\circ:M\times N&\longrightarrow k\\
(m\;,\;n)&\longmapsto m\circ n\end{split}

be a
k
-bilinear map which is
continuous in the two variables separately. For
?={\rm weak},{\rm strong}
, we say that
\circ

is a
?
-left perfect pairing

if the map

\begin{split}M&\longrightarrow{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(N,k)\\
m&\longmapsto(y\longmapsto m\circ y)\end{split}

identifies
M
with the
?
-dual of
N
. We say that
\circ

is a
?
-right perfect pairing

if
the map

\begin{split}N&\longrightarrow{\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}(M,k)\\
n&\longmapsto(x\longmapsto x\circ n)\end{split}

identifies
N
with the
?
-dual of
M
. If
\circ
is both
?
-left perfect and
?
-right perfect, we say that
\circ
is a
?
perfect pairing
.

3.3
Relevant subcategories of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}

We recall that
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\subset{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}\subset{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
is a sequence of full embeddings of categories, where the two smaller ones are quasi-abelian.
Let us denote by
{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
the full subcategory of
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
consisting of
finitely and freely
generated
k
-modules equipped with their naive

k
-canonical topology.
For any full subcategory
{\mathcal{A}}
of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
,
we consider the following full subcategories of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
:

1.

The full subcategory
{\rm colim}^{\rm u}{\mathcal{A}}
of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
consisting of
objects of the form
{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
, for a filtered inductive system
\{M_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
of objects of
{\mathcal{A}}
. Notice that
{\rm colim}^{\rm u}{\mathcal{F}}_{k}\subset{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
and that
objects of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
of the form

{\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{\alpha\in A}ke_{\alpha}

where, for any index set
A
,
ke_{\alpha}
denotes a copy of the object
k
of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, are in

{\rm colim}^{\rm u}{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
.

2.

The full subcategory
{\rm lim}\,{\mathcal{A}}
of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
consisting of
objects of the form
{\rm lim}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
, for a cofiltered projective system
\{M_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
of objects of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Objects of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
of the form

\prod_{\alpha\in A}ke_{\alpha}

where, for any index set
A
,
ke_{\alpha}
denotes a copy of the object
k
of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, are in

{\rm lim}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
.

3.

The full subcategory
{\rm lim}^{\rm can}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
of
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
consisting of
objects of the form
{\rm lim}^{\rm can}_{\alpha\in A}F_{\alpha}
, for a cofiltered projective system
\{F_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
of objects of
{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
.

4.

The full subcategory
{\rm lim}\,{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
consisting of
objects of the form
{\rm lim}_{\alpha\in A}M_{\alpha}
, for a cofiltered projective system
\{M_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
of objects of
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
.

3.4
Duals of tensor products

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
M,N
be objects of

{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
.
Then, for
?={\rm weak},{\rm strong}
, we have a natural morphism

(3.11.1)

M^{\prime}_{?}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N^{\prime}_{?}\xrightarrow{\ \ }(M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N)^{\prime}_{?}

in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
.

Proof.

For any ring
R
and any
M,N\in{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{R}
, we have a natural
R
-linear map

(3.11.2)

{\rm Hom}_{R}(M,R)\otimes_{R}{\rm Hom}_{R}(N,R)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\rm Hom}_{R}(M\otimes_{R}N,R)\;.

From Corollary
3.4
:

M^{\prime}_{?}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N^{\prime}_{?}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}(M/IM)^{\prime}_{?}\otimes_{k/I}{\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}(N/IN)^{\prime}_{?}\xrightarrow{\ \eqref{tensfunct2}\ }{\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}((M/IM)^{\prime}_{?}\otimes_{k/I}(N/IN)^{\prime}_{?})

\xrightarrow{\ \ref{tens}\ }{\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}(M/IM\otimes_{k/I}N/IN)^{\prime}_{?}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}(M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N/I(M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N))^{\prime}_{?}=(M\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}N)^{\prime}_{?}\;,

where the first arrow follows from functoriality while the second is deduced from (
3.11.2
).
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
A,B
be ring objects of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
. Then there are natural morphisms
A\xrightarrow{\ \ }A\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}B
(resp.
B\xrightarrow{\ \ }A\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}B
),
a\xrightarrow{\ \ }a\otimes 1_{B}
(resp.
b\xrightarrow{\ \ }1_{A}\otimes b
). Then, for
?={\rm weak},{\rm strong}
, we have a natural morphism

(3.12.1)

(A\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}B)^{\prime}_{?}\xrightarrow{\ \ }A^{\prime}_{?}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}B^{\prime}_{?}

in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
.

Corollary 3.13
.

Let
A,B
be (commutative unital)
k
-algebra objects of
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
. Then the morphism (
3.12.1
) is an isomorphism of (co-commutative co-unital)
k
-coalgebra objects of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
.

4
The category of
td
-spaces

4.1
Topological generalities

Let
{{\mathcal{T}}}op
be the category of topological spaces and continuous maps. For a topological space
X
we denote by
\tau=\tau_{X}
its topology,
i.e.
the family of open subsets of
X
. The category
{{\mathcal{T}}}op
is bicomplete. For a small family
\{X_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
, the coproduct
\coprod_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}
is the disjoint union of open subspaces
X^{\prime}_{\alpha}
, for
\alpha\in A
, and is equipped with continuous maps
\iota_{\alpha}:X_{\alpha}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}
which induce homeomorphisms
X_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }X^{\prime}_{\alpha}
, for any
\alpha
. We informally call
sum
the coproduct in
{{\mathcal{T}}}op
.

We recall that a continuous map
f:X\to Y
of topological spaces, with
Y
locally compact Hausdorff, is said to be

1.

proper
if, equivalently,

(a)

the preimage of every compact subset of
Y
is a compact in

X
,

(b)

f
is closed and has compact fibers.

2.

locally finite
(resp.
finite
) if it is closed and has finite fibers
(resp. and there exists an integer
n
such that the cardinality of any fiber of
f
is bounded by
n
; we then say that
the degree of
f
is
\leq n
).

Notation 4.1
.

For any topological space
X
, and for
x\in X
, the
component
of
x
is the union of all connected subsets of
X

containing
x
. The
quasi-component
of
x
is the intersection of clopen (= closed and open) subsets of
X
containing
x
.
So the quasi-component of
x
in
X
contains the component of
x
. In a compact Hausdorff topological space
X
and for any
x\in X
, the quasi-component and the component of
x
in
X
coincide. A topological space
X
is
hereditarily disconnected
(resp.
totally disconnected
) if for any
x\in X
the component (resp. the quasi-component) of
x
is
\{x\}
. A non-empty Hausdorff space
X
is
zero dimensional
if its topology admits a basis
consisting of clopen subsets. A zero dimensional space is totally disconnected hence hereditarily disconnected. A Hausdorff topological space is
paracompact
(resp. a
Lindelöf space
) if (resp. it is regular and) every open cover has a locally finite (resp. countable) open refinement. In a Lindelöf space every open cover admits a countable subcover.

Theorem 4.2
.

(
[
8
, Thm. 6.2.10]
) For a non empty locally compact paracompact space
X
the three notions are equivalent :

1.

X
is hereditarily disconnected;

2.

X
is totally disconnected;

3.

X
is zero-dimensional.

Any (resp. non empty) subspace of a hereditarily/totally disconnected (resp.
0
-dimensional) topological space is hereditarily/totally disconnected (resp.
0
-dimensional)
[
8
, 6.2.11]
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
X=\coprod_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}
be the sum of a non empty family of non empty topological spaces. Then

1.

X
is hereditarily/totally disconnected (resp.
0
-dimensional) if and only if every
X_{\alpha}
is
[
8
, 6.2.13]
.

2.

X
is paracompact (resp. locally compact) if and only if every
X_{\alpha}
is : see
[
8
, 5.1.30]
(resp. this is clear).

We are indebted to Umberto Marconi for pointing out to us the following theorem in Engelking
[
8
, 5.1.27]

Theorem 4.4
.

Every locally compact paracompact space
X
is the sum of a family of Lindelöf spaces, and conversely.

4.2
Stone spaces and
td
-spaces

Definition 4.5
.

For a topological space
X
, we let
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
denote the set of its discrete proper quotients,
i.e.
of continuous surjective maps with compact fibers
\pi_{D}:X\longrightarrow D
where
D
is a discrete topological space.

Definition 4.6
.

A compact Hausdorff space
X
satisfying the three equivalent conditions of Proposition
4.2
is called a
Stone space
.

The following statement is well-known.

Lemma 4.7
.

The following properties of a topological space
X
are equivalent:

1.

X
is a Stone space;

2.

Any open cover of
X
admits a refinement which is a finite partition of
X
in a family of open compact subspaces;

3.

X
is the limit of the projective system of its finite discrete quotients;

4.

X
is a small cofiltered limit of finite discrete spaces and surjective maps,
i.e.

X
is a profinite set.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
X
be a Stone space. The following are equivalent :

1.

X
is second countable;

2.

X
is metrizable;

3.

the family of clopen subsets of
X
is countable;

4.

X
is an inverse limit of a sequence of finite (discrete) spaces.

Definition 4.9
.

A
td
-space
is a locally compact paracompact
0
-dimensional topological space.
We let
td-{\mathcal{S}}
denote the full subcategory of
{{\mathcal{T}}}op
consisting of
td
-spaces.

Clearly, any Stone space is a
td
-space.

Lemma 4.10
.

A locally compact
0
-dimensional Lindelöf space is a countable sum of Stone subspaces, and conversely.

Proof.

In a locally compact
0
-dimensional space a basis for the topology is given by all open compact subsets. So, a locally compact
0
-dimensional space
X
admits an open cover
{\mathscr{U}}
by Stone subspaces. If moreover
X
is
Lindelöf we may assume that the cover
{\mathscr{U}}
is countable,
{\mathscr{U}}=\{U_{j}\}_{j=0,1,\dots}
. Then the sequence of open Stone subspaces
V_{j}=U_{j}-\bigcup_{i<j}U_{i}
,
j=0,1,\dots
gives a partition of
X
, as required.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

1.

Let
X=\coprod_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}
be the sum of a non empty family of non empty topological spaces. Then

X
is a
td
-space if and only if every
X_{\alpha}
is one.

2.

A
td
-space is a sum of Stone spaces and conversely.

Proof.

\mathit{1}
follows from the definition and Lemma
4.3
.

\mathit{2}
follows from Theorem
4.4
and Lemma
4.10
.
∎

4.3
Limit and colimit description

Theorem 4.12
.

The following properties of a topological space
X
are equivalent:

1.

X
is the limit

(4.12.1)

X={\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}\,D\;.

2.

X
is the limit

(4.12.2)

X={\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}\,D

of a small cofiltered projective system
{\mathscr{J}}
of discrete spaces and locally finite surjective maps.
(In this case,
{\mathscr{J}}
turns out to be a cofinal projective sub-system of
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
see Remark
4.14
below).

3.

X
is the sum

(4.12.3)

X=\coprod_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}

of a small family of Stone spaces.

4.

X
is paracompact and is the colimit of the inductive system of its open Stone subspaces.

5.

X
is paracompact and is the colimit of a small filtered inductive system of Stone spaces and open embeddings.

6.

X
is Hausdorff and any open cover of
X
can be refined by a partition of
X
consisting of open compact subspaces.

7.

X
is a
td
-space.

Proof.

\mathit{1}\Rightarrow\mathit{2}
is obvious since
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
is a
{\mathscr{J}}
as required.

To prove
\mathit{2}\Rightarrow\mathit{3}
we pick any discrete quotient of
X
,
A\in{\mathscr{J}}
. Any
\pi_{A,D}:D\to A
in
{\mathscr{J}}/A={\mathscr{J}}/{\pi_{A}}
,
is a direct sum of finite surjective maps
\pi_{A,D}^{-1}(\alpha)\to\{\alpha\}
. Then

X_{\alpha}:={\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}/A}\pi_{A,D}^{-1}(\alpha)
is a Stone space, and (
4.12.3
) holds.

To prove
\mathit{3}\Rightarrow\mathit{1}
we
give to the set
A
the discrete topology. Then the projection
\pi_{A}:X\to A
may be viewed as an object of
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
.
The morphisms
\pi_{D}:X\to D
in
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
for which there exists
\pi_{A,D}:D\to A
such that
\pi_{A}=\pi_{A,D}\circ\pi_{D}
form a cofinal projective subsystem
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)/A
in
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
. On the other hand
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)/A
is the product category
\prod_{\alpha\in A}{\mathscr{Q}}(X_{\alpha})
. For any topological space
Y
, a continuous map
f:Y\to X
determines a continuous map
\sigma=\pi_{A}\circ f:Y\to A
and a partition of
Y
in clopen fibers
\sigma^{-1}(\alpha)
, for
\alpha\in A
. So,

\begin{split}{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathcal{T}}}op}(Y,X)=&\prod_{\sigma\in{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathcal{T}}}op}(Y,A)}{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathcal{T}}}op}(\sigma^{-1}(\alpha),X_{\alpha})=\\
\prod_{\sigma\in{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathcal{T}}}op}(Y,A)}&{\rm lim}_{D_{\alpha}\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X_{\alpha})}{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathcal{T}}}op}(\sigma^{-1}(\alpha),D_{\alpha})=\\
{\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}&{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathcal{T}}}op}(Y,D)={\rm Hom}_{{{\mathcal{T}}}op}(Y,{\rm colim}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}D)\;.\end{split}

\mathit{3}\Rightarrow\mathit{4}\Rightarrow\mathit{5}
is clear.

\mathit{5}\Rightarrow\mathit{3}
By Theorem
4.4

X
is a sum of Lindelöf spaces. On the other hand,
X
is

0
-dimensional since its open Stone subspaces form a basis for the topology of
X
; so, it is a sum of
0
-dimensional Lindelöf spaces. By Lemma
4.10
,
X
is a sum of Stone subspaces.

\mathit{3}\Rightarrow\mathit{6}
follows from
\mathit{2}
of Lemma
4.7
.

\mathit{6}\Rightarrow\mathit{7}
because
\mathit{6}
is just a reformulation of the definition of a
td
-space.

\mathit{7}\Leftrightarrow\mathit{3}
is Corollary
4.11
.

∎

Remark 4.13
.

Our definition of a
td
-space is more restrictive than Ngo’s
[
17
, §1]
in that we require paracompactness. On the other hand, a
td
-space which is countable at infinity in the sense of Ngo, is precisely a
Lindelöf
td
-space in our sense
[
8
, 3.8.C (b)]
.

Remark 4.14
.

It follows from
\mathit{1}
of Theorem
4.12
that, for any
td
-space
X
and any cofinal
projective subsystem
{\mathscr{J}}
of
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
, (
4.12.2
) holds
in
{{\mathcal{T}}}op
. Conversely, any cofiltered projective subsystem
{\mathscr{J}}
of
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
such that (
4.12.2
) holds is cofinal in
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
. In fact,

{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathcal{T}}}op}(X,X)={\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}{\rm colim}_{E\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathcal{S}}}ets}(E,D)

is not empty because it contains
{\rm id}_{X}
. Therefore, for any
D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
, there must exist
E\in{\mathscr{J}}
and
an arrow
E\to D
in
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
. So,
{\mathscr{J}}
must be cofinal in
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
.
Similarly, for any cofinal
inductive subsystem
\{C_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
of
{\mathcal{K}}(X)

(4.14.1)

X={\rm colim}_{\alpha\in A}C_{\alpha}\;.

Again, any filtered inductive subsystem
\{C_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
of
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
satisfying (
4.14.1
) is necessarily cofinal in
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
. In fact, let
C
be any element of
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
. Then there exist a finite number
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
of indices in
A
such that
C\subset C_{\alpha_{1}}\bigcup\dots\bigcup C_{\alpha_{n}}
. Since
A
is filtered, there exists
\alpha\in A
such that
C_{\alpha}\supset C_{\alpha_{1}}\bigcup\dots\bigcup C_{\alpha_{n}}\supset C
.

Remark 4.15
.

It follows from
\mathit{4}
of Theorem
4.12
that a
td
-space
X
is a filtered union of open Stone subspaces. Moreover, any compact subset of
X
is contained in an open compact subset of
X
.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
X,Y
be
td
-spaces and let

(4.16.1)

X={\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}D\;\;,\;\;Y={\rm lim}_{D^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{J}}^{\prime}}D^{\prime}

(resp.

(4.16.2)

X={\rm colim}_{\alpha\in A}C_{\alpha}\;\;,\;\;Y={\rm colim}_{\beta\in B}C^{\prime}_{\beta}\;

with
C_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
,
C^{\prime}_{\beta}\in{\mathcal{K}}(Y)
)
be a representation of
X
,
Y
, as in (
4.12.2
) (resp. as in
\mathit{5}
of Theorem
4.12
).
Then

(4.16.3)

\begin{split}{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathcal{T}}}op}(X,Y)&={\rm lim}_{D^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{J}}^{\prime}}{\rm colim}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}(D,D^{\prime})=\\
{\rm lim}_{\alpha\in A}&{\rm colim}_{\beta\in B}{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathcal{T}}}op}(C_{\alpha},C^{\prime}_{\beta})\;.\end{split}

Proof.

Immediate.
∎

Corollary 4.17
.

The following properties of a topological space
X
are equivalent:

1.

X
is a
td
-space and the projective system
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
admits a cofinal sequential projective sub-system of locally finite maps
D_{n+1}\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{n+1,n}\ }D_{n}
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, where each
D_{n}
is a countable discrete set.

2.

X
is the limit

(4.17.1)

X={\rm lim}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\,D_{n}

of a sequential projective system
\{D_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of discrete spaces and locally finite surjective maps.

3.

X
is the sum

(4.17.2)

X=\coprod_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}X_{n}

of a countable family of metrizable Stone spaces.

4.

X
is a second-countable locally compact totally disconnected Hausdorff space.

5.

X
is a locally compact totally disconnected Hausdorff space whose family
\Sigma(X)
of clopen subsets is at most countable.

6.

X
is metrizable and admits a cover of open Stone subspaces.

7.

X
is the colimit of a sequence
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of metrizable Stone spaces and open embeddings.

8.

X
is Hausdorff and any open cover of
X
can be refined by a countable partition of
X
consisting of open compact subspaces.

9.

X
is a metrizable
td
-space.

10.

X
is a second countable
td
-space.

4.4
Uniform structures on a
td
-space

4.4.1
Generalities on uniform spaces.

We slightly modify the definitions and notation of
[
8
, §8.1]
on general uniform spaces
to avoid separation conditions. According to
[
18
]
our spaces would rather be
pre-uniform spaces
, but we
do not insist on this terminology, since eventually our spaces of interest will be separated.

We recall from
[
8
, §5.1]
that for any set
X
, there are two relevant relations among covers
{\mathscr{P}},{\mathscr{Q}}
of
X
. Namely,
{\mathscr{Q}}\leq{\mathscr{P}}

i.e.

{\mathscr{Q}}
is a
refinement
of
{\mathscr{P}}
meaning that for any
B\in{\mathscr{Q}}
there is
A\in{\mathscr{P}}
such that
B\subset A
, and
{\mathscr{Q}}\leq^{\ast}{\mathscr{P}}

i.e.

{\mathscr{Q}}
is a
star refinement
of
{\mathscr{P}}
meaning that for every
B\in{\mathscr{Q}}
there is an
A\in{\mathscr{P}}
such that, if
C\in{\mathscr{Q}}
and
B\cap C\neq\emptyset
, then
C\subset A
.

Remark 4.18
.

Notice that for
any two
partitions

{\mathscr{P}}
and
{\mathscr{Q}}
of a set
X
, we have

(4.18.1)

{\mathscr{Q}}\leq^{\ast}{\mathscr{P}}\Leftrightarrow{\mathscr{Q}}\leq{\mathscr{P}}\Leftrightarrow\mbox{any $B\in{\mathscr{Q}}$ is contained in some}\;\;A\in{\mathscr{P}}\;.

A uniform space
(X,\Theta)
(on the topological space
X
) may be viewed as a topological space
X
equipped with a distinguished family
\Theta
of open covers, called
uniform
, which satisfy the properties

1.

if
{\mathscr{Q}}\leq{\mathscr{P}}
and
{\mathscr{Q}}\in\Theta
, then
{\mathscr{P}}\in\Theta
;

2.

if
{\mathscr{P}},{\mathscr{Q}}\in\Theta
, there exists
{\mathscr{R}}\in\Theta
such that
{\mathscr{R}}\leq{\mathscr{P}}
and
{\mathscr{R}}\leq{\mathscr{Q}}
;

3.

for any
{\mathscr{P}}\in\Theta
there is
{\mathscr{Q}}\in\Theta
such that
{\mathscr{Q}}\leq^{\ast}{\mathscr{P}}
;

A family of open covers
\Theta
of the topological space
X
is a
basis of uniform covers
of a well-defined uniform space, still denoted by
(X,\Theta)
, on
X
if it satisfies 2,3 above, in which case an open cover
{\mathscr{P}}
of
X
is uniform iff there is a refinement
{\mathscr{Q}}\leq{\mathscr{P}}
,
{\mathscr{Q}}\in\Theta
. We say that
\Theta
