, and the uniform space
(X,\Theta)
,
define a uniformity on the topological space
X
.
Let
(X,\Theta_{X})
and
(Y,\Theta_{Y})
be uniform spaces, where
\Theta_{X}
(resp.
\Theta_{Y}
) is meant to be a basis of uniform covers on
X
(resp.
Y
). A map
f:X\to Y
defines a morphism
f:(X,\Theta_{X})\to(Y,\Theta_{Y})
of uniform spaces or is
uniformly continuous
, if
f
is continuous and, for any
{\mathscr{P}}\in\Theta_{Y}
, the cover
\{f^{-1}(B)\,|\,B\in{\mathscr{P}}\,\}
of
(X,\Theta_{X})
is uniform.

We denote by
{{\mathscr{U}}}nif
the category of uniform spaces and by

{\rm top}:{{\mathscr{U}}}nif\longrightarrow{{\mathcal{T}}}op\;\;,\;\;(X,\Theta)\longmapsto X\;,

the natural functor. There is a second functor

{\rm neat}:{{\mathscr{U}}}nif\longrightarrow{{\mathcal{T}}}op\;\;,\;\;(X,\Theta)\longmapsto(X,\Theta)_{\rm neat}\;,

where
(X,\Theta_{X})_{\rm neat}=X_{\rm neat}\in{{\mathcal{T}}}op
is the set
X
on which a basis of open neighborhoods of any point
x\in X
is given by the family

(4.18.2)

{\mathcal{U}}(x)=\{A\subset X\,|\,\exists\;{\mathscr{P}}\in\Theta_{X}\;\mbox{such that}\;A\in{\mathscr{P}}\,\}\;.

Then
(X,\Theta)\longmapsto{\rm id}_{X}
gives a natural transformation
{\rm top}\xrightarrow{\ \varphi\ }{\rm neat}
. We will say that
(X,\Theta)
is
neat
if
\varphi((X,\Theta))
is a homeomorphism.
A uniform space
(X,\Theta)
is
discrete
if
\{\{x\}|x\in X\}
is a uniform cover of
(X,\Theta)
(in particular,
X
is then discrete and
(X,\Theta)
is neat).

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
\Theta
be a basis of uniform covers for a uniformity on the set
X
. Then,
a basis of entourages for the corresponding uniformity of
X
is
\{U_{\mathscr{P}}\}_{{\mathscr{P}}\in\Theta}
where

U_{\mathscr{P}}\subset X\times X
is of the form
U_{\mathscr{P}}=\bigcup_{A\in{\mathscr{P}}}A\times A
.

Proof.

See
[
8
, Prop. 8.1.16]
.
∎

Remark 4.20
.

Let
R
be any linearly topologized ring (resp. let
M
be any linearly topologized
R
-module).
Then
R
(resp.
M
) carries a canonical uniformity
\Theta_{R}
(resp.
\Theta_{M}
) compatible with the given topology, called the
R
-linear
uniformity
of
R
(resp.
M
). A basis
of uniform covers of
\Theta_{R}
(resp.
\Theta_{M}
) is given by the covers of the form

{\mathscr{P}}_{J}=\{x+J\}_{x\in R}
for
J\in{\mathcal{P}}(R)
(resp.
{\mathscr{P}}_{P}=\{x+P\}_{x\in M}
for
{P\in{\mathcal{P}}_{R}(M)}
). Such covers
are in
(1,1)
-correspondence with the discrete quotients

\pi_{J}:R\longrightarrow R/J\;\;\;\mbox{(resp.}\;\pi_{P}:M\longrightarrow M/P\;\mbox{)}

for
J\in{\mathcal{P}}(R)
(resp. for
{P\in{\mathcal{P}}_{R}(M)}
).

For any uniform space
(X,\Theta_{X})
, a uniformly continuous
R
-valued function on
(X,\Theta_{X})
is a morphism
(X,\Theta_{X})\to(R,\Theta_{R})
of uniform spaces. Notice that for
R
as before, and
R
-linearly topologized
R
-modules
M
,
N
, any continuous
R
-linear map
M\to N
is uniformly continuous.

4.4.2
td
-uniformities.

We now describe a full subcategory of
{{\mathscr{U}}}nif
consisting of certain uniform spaces whose associated topological space is a
td
-space.
Since a
td
-space
X
is obviously a Tychonoff space, there exist uniform structures on
X

[
8
, Thm. 8.1.20]
.

Definition 4.21
.

Let
X
be a topological space. A partition
{\mathscr{P}}
of
X
into a family of open Stone subspaces is called a
td
-partition
of
X
. A collection
\Theta
of
td
-partitions of
X
which is a basis of uniform covers for some uniformity on
X
, will be called
a basis of
td
-partitions on
X
.

Notation 4.22
.

Clearly, we may identify any
td
-partition
{\mathscr{P}}
of
(X,\tau)
with the corresponding map

(4.22.1)

\begin{split}\pi_{{\mathscr{P}}}:X&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{P}}\\
x&\longmapsto[x]\end{split}

where
[x]
, a compact open subset of
X
, denotes the element of
{\mathscr{P}}
containing
x\in X
. This map is clearly continuous and proper if
{\mathscr{P}}
is equipped with the discrete topology, so that
we identify a
td
-partition
{\mathscr{P}}
with the corresponding element
\pi_{\mathscr{P}}\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
.

A basis
\Theta
of
td
-partitions on
X
may be regarded equivalently as

1.

a projective cofiltered subsystem
{\mathscr{J}}
of
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
;

2.

a family
\Theta
of
td
-partitions of
X
cofiltered by the relation
\leq
of subsection
4.4.1
.

We write
{\mathscr{J}}\leftrightarrow\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}
for the previous correspondence.

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
X
be a
td
-space and let
\Theta=\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}
be a basis
of
td
-partitions of
X
where
{\mathscr{J}}
is a cofiltered projective subsystem of
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
. Then
(X,\Theta)
is neat if and only if one of the following equivalent conditions is satisfied

1.

for any
x\in X
, a fundamental system of neighborhoods of
x
in
X
is given by the subsets
A\subset X
such that

x\in A\in{\mathscr{P}}
, for some
{\mathscr{P}}\in\Theta
;

2.

the family of fibers of maps
\pi_{D}:X\xrightarrow{\ \ }D
in
{\mathscr{J}}
is a basis for the topology of
X
;

3.

any point
x\in X
has a compact open neighborhood
U
such that the subsystem

(\pi_{|U}:U\xrightarrow{\ \ }\pi(U))_{\pi\in{\mathscr{J}}}\subset{\mathscr{Q}}(U)

is cofinal in
{\mathscr{Q}}(U)
.

Proof.

Easy.
∎

Definition 4.24
.

An object
(X,\Theta)
of
{{\mathscr{U}}}nif
is a
td
-uniform space
if
X
is a
td
-space and

\Theta=\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}
is a basis of
td
-partitions on
X
as in Proposition
4.23
. We sometimes shorten

(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
into
(X,{\mathscr{J}})
.
The full subcategory of

{{\mathscr{U}}}nif
consisting of
td
-uniform spaces, will be denoted by
td-{\mathcal{U}}
.

Proposition 4.25
.

Let
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
,
(Y,\Theta_{\mathscr{H}})
be objects of
td-{\mathcal{U}}
.
A morphism

(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})\xrightarrow{\ \ }(Y,\Theta_{\mathscr{H}})

in
td-{\mathcal{U}}
is a continuous map
X\xrightarrow{\ f\ }Y
such that, for any
\pi_{E}:Y\xrightarrow{\ \ }E
in
{\mathscr{H}}
, there exists
\pi_{D}:X\xrightarrow{\ \ }D
and a map of sets
f_{D,E}:D\xrightarrow{\ \ }E
such that
\pi_{E}\circ f=f_{D,E}\circ\pi_{D}
.

Proof.

Clear.
∎

Remark 4.26
.

A topological space
X
on which there exists a
td
-partition (
a fortiori
a
td
-uniformity)
is necessarily a
td
-space.
Conversely, any neat uniform structure
(X,\Theta)
on a
td
-space
X
may be refined to a neat

td
-uniformity
(X,\Lambda)
, as follows.
Let
{\mathscr{Q}}\in\Theta
be a uniform cover in
\Theta
.
Since
X
is a
td
-space, for any
x\in X
, we may pick a compact open neighborhood
K_{x}
of
x
in
X
, such that for some
C\in{\mathscr{Q}}
,
x\in K_{x}\subset C
.
The cover
{\mathscr{K}}:=\{K_{x}\}_{x\in X}
refines
{\mathscr{Q}}
. Let

X
be the sum of a family of Stone spaces as in (
4.12.3
). Then, for any
\alpha\in A
such that
x\in X_{\alpha}
, let

K_{x,\alpha}=X_{\alpha}\cap K_{x}

So,

{\mathscr{P}}=\{K_{x,\alpha}\,|\,\alpha\in A\;\mbox{and}\;x\in X_{\alpha}\,\}

is a cover on
X
by open Stone subspaces which refines
{\mathscr{Q}}
.
Then, for any
\alpha\in A
, there is a finite index set
F_{\alpha}=\{1,2,\dots,n_{\alpha}\}
and a family
\{K_{\alpha,j}\}_{j\in F_{\alpha}}
in
{\mathscr{P}}
, such that

X_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{j\in F_{\alpha}}K_{\alpha,j}\;.

We may then set

U_{\alpha,1}=K_{\alpha,1}\;,\;U_{\alpha,2}=K_{\alpha,2}-K_{\alpha,2}\cap U_{\alpha,1}\;,\;\dots\;,\;U_{\alpha,n_{\alpha}}=K_{\alpha,n_{\alpha}}-K_{\alpha,n_{\alpha}}\cap U_{\alpha,n_{\alpha}-1}\;.

Finally, we have obtained a
td
-partition

{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}:=\{U_{\alpha,j}\;|\;\alpha\in A\;,\;j\in F_{\alpha}\;\}

of
X
which refines (
{\mathscr{P}}
hence)
{\mathscr{Q}}
. Notice that there may not exist a further uniform cover
{\mathscr{Q}}^{\prime}\in\Theta
which refines

{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}
. See a counterexample in the next remark
4.27
.

Remark 4.27
.

Let
D
be an infinite discrete space. We consider the family
{\mathcal{D}}
of partitions
{\mathscr{D}}
of
D
into a finite set

\{D_{1},\dots,D_{n},D_{\infty}\}
of subsets
D_{i}\subset D
, where
D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{n}
are finite subsets of
D
. Then
{\mathcal{D}}
is a basis of uniform covers for a neat uniformity of
D
(that is

(D,{\mathcal{D}})_{\rm neat}=D
, equipped with the
discrete topology). Any
td
-refinement of the cover
{\mathscr{D}}
is necessarily infinite, hence cannot be refined by any
{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}
.

Remark 4.28
.

By
[
8
, Ex. 8.2.B (c)]
,
[
9
, Chap. II, §2, N. 7]

in the category
{{\mathscr{U}}}nif
projective limits are representable.
For a projective system
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\bullet}=((X_{\alpha},\Theta_{{\mathscr{J}}_{\alpha}}))_{\alpha\in A}
in
td-{\mathcal{U}}
, the limit

(X,\Theta):={\rm lim}_{\alpha}{\mathfrak{X}}_{\alpha}

of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\bullet}
in
{{\mathcal{U}}}nif
is obtained as follows. We set
X={\rm lim}_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}
in
{{\mathcal{T}}}op
, so that

X
is a
td
-space. For any
\alpha
, let
X\xrightarrow{\ f_{\alpha}\ }X_{\alpha}
be the projection. Then any proper quotient map
\pi_{\alpha,D}:X_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\ \ }D
in
{\mathscr{J}}_{\alpha}
induces a proper quotient map
\pi_{\alpha,D}\circ f_{\alpha}:X\xrightarrow{\ \ }D
to the discrete space
D
. The union
{\mathscr{J}}=\bigcup_{\alpha}{\mathscr{J}}_{\alpha}
is a projective sub-system of
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
, and
(X,\Theta)=(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
. We conclude that
td-{\mathcal{U}}
is closed under limits in
{{\mathcal{U}}}nif
.

In particular,
for any
td
-uniform space
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
and any
\pi_{D}:X\xrightarrow{\ \ }D
in
{\mathscr{J}}
, let
{\mathscr{J}}_{D}=\{\pi_{D}\}
and let us write
\Theta_{D}
for
\Theta_{{\mathscr{J}}_{D}}
.
Then
(X,\Theta_{D})=(X,\{\pi_{D}\})
and

(4.28.1)

(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})={\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}(X,\Theta_{D})={\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}(X,\{\pi_{D}\})\;,

in the category
td-{\mathcal{U}}
.

Definition 4.29
.

A neat
td
-uniform space
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
as in
Definition
4.24
is
metrizable
if the topological space
X
is metrizable.

Proposition 4.30
.

A neat
td
-uniform space
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
is metrizable if and only if
{\mathscr{J}}
admits a countable
cofinal subsystem
{\mathscr{J}}^{\prime}
such that, for any
X\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{D}\ }D
in
{\mathscr{J}}^{\prime}
,
D
is countable.

Proof.

Clear.
∎

Remark 4.31
.

It follows from Exercise 8.1.B (b) of
[
8
]
that, for any
td
-space
X
(more generally, for any Tychonoff space), there is a finest uniformity
\Theta_{{\rm univ}}
of
X
compatible with the topology of
X
. It is called the
universal uniformity
(on the Tychonoff space
X
). Since any uniformity on a
td
-space compatible with the topology is refined by a
td
-uniformity
we have

(4.31.1)

(X,\Theta_{{\rm univ}})=(X,\Theta_{{\mathscr{Q}}(X)})\;.

Lemma 4.32
.

A uniform
td
-space is complete.

Proof.

In fact, for any Cauchy filter
{\mathcal{G}}
for
(X,\Theta)
and any
{\mathscr{P}}\in\Theta
, there exists
C\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
C\times C\subset U_{\mathscr{P}}
. So,
C\times C\subset\bigcup_{A\in{\mathcal{P}}}A\times A
and, if there exist
A_{1}\neq A_{2}
in
{\mathcal{P}}
such that
C\cap A_{i}\neq\emptyset
for
i=1,2
, then
(C\cap A_{1})\times(C\cap A_{2})

cannot be contained in
U_{\mathscr{P}}
. Therefore, there exists
A\in{\mathscr{P}}
such that
C\subset A
. But then one of the elements of
{\mathcal{G}}
, namely
A
, is compact.
Then
\bigcap_{G\in{\mathcal{G}}}\overline{G}\neq\emptyset
, since all
\overline{G}\cap A
have the finite intersection property in the compact subset
A
. So,
\bigcap_{F\in{\mathcal{G}}}\overline{G}
consists of a single point
x\in X
, and the filter
{\mathcal{G}}
converges to
x
.
∎

Remark 4.33
.

The
td
-space
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
carries a natural uniformity for which a basis of uniform covers is
\Theta:=\{{\mathscr{P}}_{h}\}_{h}
, for
h=0,1,2,\dots
, where
{\mathscr{P}}_{h}
consists of the family of balls of the same radius
p^{h}
:

{\mathscr{P}}_{h}=\{a+p^{h}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\,|\,a\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\;\}\;.

The corresponding cofiltered projective system
of discrete quotients of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, by our dictionary, is

\pi_{h}:{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}/p^{h}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\;,\;h=0,1,2,\dots\;.

Then
{\mathscr{J}}:=(\pi_{h})_{h\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
\Theta=\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}
clearly satisfies (
4.28.1
). Moreover,

({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
4.23
, so that
it is neat,
i.e.

({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})_{\rm neat}
is

{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
with its usual topology.
But
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
does not coincide with
{\rm lim}_{h}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}/p^{h}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
in
{{\mathcal{T}}}op
because
{\mathscr{J}}
is not cofinal in
{\mathscr{Q}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. For example, the
td
-partition

\{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\}\bigcup\{x+p^{-v_{p}(x)}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\,|\,x\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}-{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\}

of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}

cannot be refined by any
\pi_{h}
as before. Then formula (
4.12.2
) does not hold. Still, we have

({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})={\rm lim}_{h}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathscr{P}}_{h})

in
td-{\mathcal{U}}
.

Notation 4.34
.

Let
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
be a
td
-uniform space.
A discrete quotient
\pi_{D}:X\to D
in
{\mathscr{J}}
, (resp. any fiber
\pi_{D}^{-1}(d)
, for
d\in D
), will also be called a
radius
(resp. a
ball of radius
D
) of

(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
, and the open subsets of
X
of the form
\pi_{D}^{-1}(A)
, for any
A\subset D
, will be called
uniformly measurable subsets of radius
D
.
They form a boolean subalgebra
\Sigma_{D}(X)
of
{\mathfrak{P}}(X)
. The union of
\Sigma_{D}(X)
, for
D\in{\mathscr{J}}
, is the boolean subalgebra

\Sigma_{\mathscr{J}}(X)\subset{\mathfrak{P}}(X)
of
uniformly measurable subsets of
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
. For
U\subset X
,
\chi_{U}
is a
uniformly continuous map of
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
to
k
if and only if
U\subset X
is a uniformly measurable subset of
X
.

Remark 4.35
.

A topological ring
k
as in section
2.1

is not locally compact in general, hence is not a
td
-space. In particular its canonical
k
-linear uniformity
\Theta_{k}
is not, in general, a
td
-uniformity.

Proposition 4.36
.

Let
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
be a
td
-uniform space. For any discrete uniform space
E
, a continuous map
f:X\to E
is uniformly continuous on
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
if and only if
f
factors via some
X\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{D}\ }D
, with
D\in{\mathscr{J}}
. Therefore, if

(Y,\Theta_{\mathscr{H}})
is a second object of
td-{\mathcal{U}}
,

(4.36.1)

{\rm Hom}_{td-{\mathcal{U}}}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),(Y,\Theta_{\mathscr{H}}))={\rm lim}_{E\in{\mathscr{H}}}{\rm colim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}(D,E)\;.

Proof.

Immediate.
∎

Remark 4.37
.

Let
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
,
(Y,\Theta_{\mathscr{H}})
be objects of
td-{\mathcal{U}}
, and assume
\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}=\Theta_{{\rm univ}}
, the finest uniformity compatible with the topology of
X
. Then
[
8
, 8.1.C (a)]

(4.37.1)

{\rm Hom}_{td-{\mathcal{U}}}((X,\Theta_{{\rm univ}}),(Y,\Theta_{\mathscr{H}}))={\rm Hom}_{td-{\mathcal{S}}}(X,Y)\;.

5
Continuous functions on a
td
-space

5.1
Continuous
and uniformly continuous
functions

We fix the topological ring
k
as in section
2.1
.

Notation 5.1
.

For any topological space
X
,
we denote by
{\rm Loc}(X,k)
the
k
-module of locally constant (automatically continuous) functions
X\longrightarrow k
.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
X
be a
td
-space
(resp. let
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
be as in Definition
4.24
).
We denote by
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
(resp.

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(X,k)={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
) the
k
-module of continuous (resp. uniformly continuous)
functions
X\to k
, equipped with the topology
of uniform convergence on compact subsets of
X
(resp. of uniform convergence on
X
). We also denote by
{\mathscr{C}}^{\circ}(X,k)
the
k
-canonical module
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)^{\rm can}
.

Remark 5.3
.

In the situation of Definition
5.2
, it is clear that both rings
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
and
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(X,k)
are linearly topologized,
i.e.
admit a basis of neighborhoods of 0 consisting of open ideals. It follows from Remark
4.15
that a basis of open submodules of
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
is the family of ideals
\{U(C,I)\}_{C,I}
indexed by open compact subsets
C
of
X
and
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
, where

(5.3.1)

U(C,I)=\{f\in{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\,:\,f(x)\in I\,,\,\forall x\in C\,\}\;.

If
X
is a metrizable
td
-space, then
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
.

A basis of open submodules of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(X,k)
is the family of ideals
\{{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(X,I)\}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}
. It follows from the definition that there is a natural continuous injection

(5.3.2)

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\;.

Remark 5.4
.

It follows from Remark
4.37
that

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{{\rm univ}}),k)={\mathscr{C}}^{\circ}(X,k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;,

i.e.
coincides with
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)^{\rm for}
equipped with the naive
k
-canonical topology.

We have

(5.4.1)

{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)={\rm lim}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X),I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k/I)

where
{\mathscr{C}}(C,k/I)
is discrete and the limit is taken in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
.
Therefore

(5.4.2)

{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\mathscr{C}}(X,k/I)

and

(5.4.3)

{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)={\rm lim}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\;.

We also observe that

(5.4.4)

{\mathscr{C}}^{\circ}(X,k)={\rm lim}^{\rm can}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\;.

A discrete space
D
may be viewed as a uniform space in our sense only if it is equipped with the discrete uniformity

\Theta_{D}
. Then by
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)
we understand

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((D,\Theta_{D}),k)={\mathscr{C}}^{\circ}(D,k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.

We have

(5.4.5)

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}({\prod}_{x\in D}(k/I)\chi_{x}^{(D)})^{\rm dis\,}={\prod}_{x\in D}^{\rm can}k\chi_{x}^{(D)}\;.

So,
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)=k^{D}
equipped with the naive canonical topology.
By (
4.36.1
), for any uniform
td
-space
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
we have

(5.4.6)

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
C
be a Stone space. Then

(5.5.1)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}&{\mathscr{C}}(C,k/I)^{\rm dis\,}={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)}(k/I)^{F}=\\
&{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)}k^{(F,{\rm u})}\;,\end{split}

where
{\mathscr{C}}(C,k/I)
is a discrete
k/I
-module and any
F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)
is a finite discrete space.
In particular,

1.

{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
;

2.

{\rm Loc}(C,k)
is dense in
{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)
;

3.

For any uniformity
\Theta
on
C
we have

(5.5.2)

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((C,\Theta),k)={\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\;.

Proof.

(
5.5.1
) is clear.

\mathit{1}.
Follows from (
5.5.1
) and Remark
2.4
.

\mathit{2}.
Follows from (
5.5.1
) and the fact that in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}

{\rm Loc}(C,k)={\rm colim}_{F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)}k^{(F)}\;.

\mathit{3}.
The only non trivial part is (
5.5.2
) which is well known and follows from the fact that, if
\Theta=\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}
, then, for any
\pi_{D}:C\to D
in
{\mathscr{J}}
,
D
is finite so that
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((D,\Theta_{D}),k)={\mathscr{C}}(D,k)=k^{D}=k^{(D)}
with the
k
-canonical topology.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

For any
td
-space
X
,

{\rm Loc}(X,k)
is dense in
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
.

Proof.

For any
C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, any
f\in{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
, and any
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
, there is a finite
td
-partition
\{U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\}
of
C
such that
f(x)-f(y)\in I
as soon as
x,y\in U_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
. Let
{\mathscr{P}}
be a
td
-partition of
X
such that, for any
1\leq i\leq n
, the elements of
{\mathscr{P}}
contained in
U_{i}
cover
U_{i}
. Then, for any
i
, let
x_{i}\in U_{i}
and let

\psi=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})\chi_{U_{i}}^{(X)}\in{\rm Loc}(X,k)\;.

Then
f-\psi\in U(C,I)
. Therefore
{\rm Loc}(X,k)
is dense in
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
.
∎

Corollary 5.7
.

Let
D
be a discrete topological space. Then from (
5.4.3
) we deduce

(5.7.1)

{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)={\rm lim}_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}(D)}{\mathscr{C}}(F,k)\;.

Explicitly

(5.7.2)

{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)=\prod_{x\in D}k\chi^{(D)}_{x}\;.

Proposition 5.8
.

For any
D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
the pull-back of functions induces a closed embedding

\pi_{D}^{\ast}:{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\;\;,\;\;f\longmapsto f\circ\pi_{D}\;.

Proof.

The map
\pi_{D}^{\ast}
is clearly injective. The topology of
{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)
viewed as a subset of
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
is the topology of uniform convergence on compact open subsets, so that it is a subspace of
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
. Since
{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)
is complete, it is a closed subspace of
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
.
∎

For any
td
-space
X
and for
\pi_{D}:X\longrightarrow D
in
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
,

{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)
identifies to the sub-object

{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)=\{f:X\to k\,:\,\forall\,x\in D\,,\,f\,\mbox{is constant on}\,\pi_{D}^{-1}(x)\,\}

of
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
. Because of (
4.12.1
)
it follows from Proposition
4.16

that, in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}
,

(5.8.1)

{\rm colim}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)={\rm Loc}(X,k)\;,

while, in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
,

(5.8.2)

{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)={\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\;.

For
D
discrete, the natural morphism

(5.8.3)

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((D,\Theta_{D}),k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)

coincides with

(5.8.4)

{\prod}_{x\in D}^{\rm can}k\chi_{x}^{(D)}\longrightarrow{\prod}_{x\in D}k\chi_{x}^{(D)}

hence is bijective.

5.2
Functions with almost compact support
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
be a
td
-uniform space. For any compact open subset
C\subset X
the continuous
k
-linear map

i_{C,X}:{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)\;,

sending
f\in{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)
to its extension by
0
to
X
is an isomorphism onto a
direct summand of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
in the category
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
. The complement of
{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)
in

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
is
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X-C,\Theta_{\mathscr{H}}),k)
where a basis of uniform covers of
(X-C,\Theta_{\mathscr{H}})
consists of those
td
-partitions of
X-C
which, together with a
td
-partition of the compact open
C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, form a basis of uniform covers of
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
.

Proof.

The partition
X=C\,\dot{\cup}\,(X-C)
is a
td
-partition of
X
which we identify to the discrete quotient

\pi_{D}:X\to D=\{C,X-C\}
. We may replace
{\mathscr{J}}
by its cofinal subsystem
{\mathscr{J}}^{\prime}
of quotients
\pi_{D^{\prime}}:X\to D^{\prime}
which factor

\pi_{D}
as
\pi_{D,D^{\prime}}\circ\pi_{D^{\prime}}
. Any such
\pi_{D^{\prime}}:X\to D^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{J}}^{\prime}
decomposes into the sum of

(\pi_{D^{\prime}})_{|C}=:\pi_{1}:C\to D_{1}\in{\mathscr{J}}_{1}\subset{\mathscr{Q}}(C)
and

(\pi_{D^{\prime}})_{|X-C}=:\pi_{2}:X-C\to D_{2}\in{\mathscr{J}}_{2}\subset{\mathscr{Q}}(X-C)
.
Then
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})=(X,\Theta_{{\mathscr{J}}^{\prime}})
is the sum of the 2 uniform spaces

(X,\Theta_{{\mathscr{J}}^{\prime}})=(C,\Theta_{{\mathscr{J}}_{1}})\coprod(X-C,\Theta_{{\mathscr{J}}_{2}})\;.

We conclude that

(5.9.1)

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((C,\Theta_{{\mathscr{J}}_{1}}),k)\bigoplus{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X-C,\Theta_{{\mathscr{J}}_{2}}),k)\;.

∎

Remark 5.10
.

In this particular case, the subspace topology of

{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\subset{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
is the topology of uniform convergence on
C
, so that it is the naive canonical topology of
{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)
.
But in general this is not the case. Namely, if
M\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
,
i.e.
if
M
is complete in its naive canonical topology, a closed
k
-linear subspace
N\subset M
does not necessarily carry its naive
k
-linear topology, but a weaker one. On the other hand, as recalled in Remark
2.2
,
N
is complete in its naive
k
-canonical topology, hence the morphism
N^{\rm can}\to N
is bijective.
So, in general,
N^{\rm can}\to M
, but not
N\to M
, is a strict monomorphism in
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
.

Definition 5.11
.

We define the space of continuous functions
X\to k
with
almost compact support
, as

(5.11.1)

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k):={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\subset C^{\prime}}({\mathscr{C}}(C,k),i_{C,C^{\prime}})\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;,

where
C,C^{\prime}
vary over the family of compact open subsets of
X
and, for
f\in{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)
,
i_{C,C^{\prime}}(f)\in{\mathscr{C}}(C^{\prime},k)
is the extension of
f
by
0
to
C^{\prime}
. We will denote by

i_{C}:{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)

the canonical morphism called
extension by
0
(from
C
) to
X
.

Remark 5.12
.

For any
td
-space
X
let
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm cs}(X,k)
be the
k
-submodule of
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
consisting of functions with compact support. Then

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)
is the completion of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm cs}(X,k)
for the
k
-canonical topology,
i.e.
for the topology of uniform convergence on
X
.

Remark 5.13
.

For any
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
,
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k/I)
is discrete and, by definition of
{\rm colim}^{\rm u}
,

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm cs}(X,k/I)\;.

Corollary 5.14
.

For
D
discrete,

(5.14.1)

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm cs}(D,k)={\rm colim}_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}(D)}({\mathscr{C}}(F,k),i_{F,F^{\prime}})=\bigoplus_{x\in D}k\chi_{x}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}\;,

(5.14.2)

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}(D)}({\mathscr{C}}(F,k),i_{F,F^{\prime}})=\bigoplus^{\rm u}_{x\in D}k\chi_{x}\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.

Corollary 5.15
.

For any uniform structure
\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}
on
(X,\tau)
,
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)
is a subspace of

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
.
The natural morphism

(5.15.1)

i_{\rm acs}:{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)

is a strict monomorphism in the quasi-abelian category
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
.
There is a sequence of injective
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
-morphisms

(5.15.2)

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ i_{\rm acs}\ }{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)

such that the natural composite morphism is a dense injection.

Proof.

The morphism
i_{\rm acs}
is obtained as follows. One first considers the colimit

{\rm colim}_{C\subset C^{\prime}}({\mathscr{C}}(C,k),i_{C,C^{\prime}})\;,

in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}
which is the
k
-submodule of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm cs}(X,k)
of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)^{\rm for}
.
The image of
i_{\rm acs}
in
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
coincides set-theoretically with the closure of the set-theoretic image of

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm cs}(X,k)
in
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
, but it is equipped with the
k
-canonical topology which is finer than the subspace topology induced by
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
. So,

i_{\rm acs}:{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
is a strict monomorphism in
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
.
This proves the first part of the Corollary.

Taking into account (
5.3.2
), we are only left to prove that the composite morphism
\varphi:{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
in (
5.15.2
)
has dense image.
For any compact open
C\subset X
, we have a morphism

(5.15.3)

\begin{split}\pi_{C}:{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\\
f&\longmapsto\varphi(f)_{|C}\end{split}

and the morphism
\varphi
is the limit of the projective system
\{\pi_{C}\}_{C}
.
Now, for any
C
, the map
\pi_{C}
is surjective because it admits the canonical right inverse
{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\xrightarrow{\ i_{C}\ }{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)
, so that
\varphi
has dense image.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

If
k
is discrete the
k
-module underlying
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)
identifies with
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm cs}(X,k)

equipped with the discrete topology.
For general
k
, the
k
-module underlying
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)
is the submodule of
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
consisting of uniform limits on
X
of compactly supported continuous functions
X\to k
. It may be described as the

k
-submodule of
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
of continuous functions
f:X\to k
which satisfy the following property (where
(ACS)
stands for “almost compact support”)

(ACS)

For any
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
, there exists a compact subset
Z_{I}\subset X
such that
f(x)\in I
, for any
x\in X-Z_{I}
.

Of course, in
(ACS)
we might as well have imposed that
Z_{I}
be a compact
open
subset of
X
.

Proposition 5.17
.

For any
td
-space
X
we have

(5.17.1)

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\;.

Proof.

We have by Remark
5.13

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm cs}(X,k/I)\;.

On the other hand, by (
4.12.1
), for any
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
we have in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k/I}

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm cs}(X,k/I)={\rm colim}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm cs}(D,k/I)\;.

So,

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm cs}(X,k/I)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\rm colim}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm cs}(D,k/I)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\;.

∎

5.3
Summary of results on rings of continuous functions

The first two corollaries have already been proven and the third is obvious.

Corollary 5.18
.

If
X=C
is a Stone space then all
morphisms in (
5.15.2
) are isomorphisms and (see Lemma
5.5
)

(5.18.1)

{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)}{\mathscr{C}}(F,k)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)}k^{(F,{\rm u})}\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}

where
F
varies over finite discrete quotients of
C
.
If
X=D
is discrete and the uniformity on
D
is understood to be the discrete uniformity, (
5.15.2
) becomes

(5.18.2)

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)={\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{x\in D}k\chi_{x}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)={\prod}_{x\in D}^{\rm can}k\chi_{x}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)={\prod}_{x\in D}k\chi_{x}\;.

Notice that the first morphism is a strict monomorphism in the quasi-abelian category
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
, while the second is bijective. The composite morphism has dense image.

We summarize our results on
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
and
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(X,k)
.

Corollary 5.19
.

Let
X
be a
td
-space. Then

1.

(see (
5.4.3
))

{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)={\rm lim}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)={\rm lim}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)}k^{(F,{\rm u})}\in{\rm lim}\,{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.

2.

(see (
5.8.2
) and (
5.7.2
))

{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}{\prod}_{x\in D}k\chi^{(D)}_{x}\;.

3.

(Definition (
5.11
))

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.

4.

(see (
5.17.1
))

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.

Corollary 5.20
.

Assume
X
admits a decomposition as a sum of Stone subspaces
\{X_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
as in (
4.12.3
). Then

{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)={\prod}_{\alpha\in A}{\mathscr{C}}(X_{\alpha},k)\;.

Remark 5.21
.

If
X
is a metrizable
td
-space, then

{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\in{\rm lim}_{\mathbb{N}}\,{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\subset{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}\;,

is a sequential limit of
k
-canonical modules.

Corollary 5.22
.

We now assume that
X
as in Corollary
5.19
is also given a
td
-uniform structure
\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}
as in Definition
5.2
. Then

1.

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k/I)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.

2.

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\prod}_{x\in D}^{\rm can}k\chi_{x}^{(D)}\;.

3.

The natural morphism

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)

is injective and has dense image.

Proof.

\mathit{1}.
This follows from the definition. Namely,
f:X\to k
is uniformly continuous for the uniformity
\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}
if and only if for any
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)

there exists a discrete quotient
D_{I}\in{\mathscr{J}}
of

X
such that
f_{I}:=f\mod I:X\to k/I
factors through the projection
\pi_{D_{I}}:X\to D_{I}
. Conversely, given a coherent system
(f_{I})_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}
of
functions
f_{I}:X\to k/I
such that
f_{I}
factors through
\pi_{D_{I}}:X\to D_{I}
and that, if
I\subset J
,
f_{J}=f_{I}\mod J
,
the sequence
(f_{I})_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}
converges uniformly on
X
to a uniformly continuous function
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
.
It is clear that the topology of uniform convergence on
X
on
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
is precisely the weak topology of the projections

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(X,k)\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(X,k/I)
,
f\mapsto f\mod I
, to the discrete
k/I
-module
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(X,k/I)
, for
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
.

\mathit{2}.
We consider the first equality.
Notice that, if
k
is discrete,

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(X,k)
consists of the
k
-module of maps
f:X\to k

which are constant on the fibers of some projection
\pi_{D}:X\to D
, for
D\in{\mathscr{J}}
,
equipped with the discrete topology. So, in that case

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)

and the result holds true. Then

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k/I)=

{\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k/I)=

{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)\;.

For the second equality, we just replace
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)
with the central term of (
5.18.2
)

\mathit{3}.
Has been proven already. Injectivity follows from the definition (
5.3.2
) and density follows from the last assertion of Corollary
5.15
.
∎

Corollary 5.23
.

Let
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
be as in Corollary
5.22
and let
\{X_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
be a partition of
X
associated to a projection
\pi_{A}:X\to A
in
{\mathscr{J}}
. Then

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)={\prod}_{\alpha\in A}^{\rm can}{\mathscr{C}}(X_{\alpha},k)

Proof.

We have

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(X,k)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(X,k/I)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}({\prod}_{\alpha\in A}{\mathscr{C}}(X_{\alpha},k/I))^{\rm dis\,}={\prod}_{\alpha\in A}^{\rm can}{\mathscr{C}}(X_{\alpha},k)\;.

∎

5.4
Tensor products of rings of continuous functions

Proposition 5.24
.

Notation as in Definition
5.2
.
For any pair of
td
-spaces
X,Y
, we have a morphism in the category
{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm u}_{k}
,

(5.24.1)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{L}}^{(X,Y)}:{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\otimes_{k}{\mathscr{C}}(Y,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(X\times Y,k)\\
f\otimes_{k}g&\longmapsto\left((x,y)\mapsto f(x)g(y)\right)\;.\end{split}

1.

For any pair of Stone spaces
C,C^{\prime}
, the map (
5.24.1
) for
X=C
and
Y=C^{\prime}
,
extends to an isomorphism

(5.24.2)

{\mathcal{L}}^{(C,C^{\prime})}:{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}{\mathscr{C}}(C^{\prime},k)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }{\mathscr{C}}(C\times C^{\prime},k)

of ring-objects of
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
.

2.

For any pair of
td
-spaces
X,Y
, the isomorphisms
{\mathcal{L}}^{(C,C^{\prime})}

of (
5.24.2
)
for compact opens
C
of
X
and
C^{\prime}
of
Y
, respectively,
determine an isomorphism

(5.24.3)

{\mathcal{L}}^{(X,Y)}_{\rm acs}:{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(Y,k)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X\times Y,k)

of (non-unital) ring-objects of
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
.

3.

For any pair of
td
-spaces
X,Y
, the map (
5.24.1
)
extends to an isomorphism of ring-objects of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}

(5.24.4)

{\mathcal{L}}^{(X,Y)}:{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}{\mathscr{C}}(Y,k)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }{\mathscr{C}}(X\times Y,k)\;.

4.

For any pair
D,E
of discrete spaces, equipped with their discrete uniformity, the map (
5.24.1
)
induces a natural injective morphism in
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}

(5.24.5)

{\mathcal{L}}^{(D,E)}_{\rm unif}:{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm unif}(E,k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D\times E,k)\;.

5.

For any pair of uniform
td
-spaces
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
,
(Y,\Theta_{\mathscr{H}})
, let
{\mathscr{J}}\times{\mathscr{H}}
be the directed set of

\pi_{D\times E}=\pi_{D}\times\pi_{E}:X\times Y\to D\times E\;,

for
\pi_{D}\in{\mathscr{J}}
and
\pi_{E}\in{\mathscr{H}}
. Then
the natural injective morphisms (
5.24.5
)
induce an injective morphism

(5.24.6)

{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm unif}^{(X,Y)}:{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((Y,\Theta_{\mathscr{H}}),k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X\times Y,\Theta_{{\mathscr{J}}\times{\mathscr{H}}}),k)

of ring-objects of
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
.

Proof.

\mathit{1}.

Let
C
and
C^{\prime}
be Stone spaces. Then,
{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)
(resp.
{\mathscr{C}}(C^{\prime},k)
, resp.
{\mathscr{C}}(C\times C^{\prime},k)
) is a colimit in
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
of free
k
-modules of finite type endowed with the naive canonical topology
of the form
{\mathscr{C}}(F,k)
(resp.
{\mathscr{C}}(G,k)
, resp.
{\mathscr{C}}(F\times G,k)
), where
F
(resp.
G
) is a finite quotient of
C
(resp.
C^{\prime}
). Since, by Proposition
2.15
,
\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}
commutes with colimits in
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
, we are reduced to the case
C=F
,
C^{\prime}=G
finite discrete spaces. In this case the map

{\mathcal{L}}:{\mathscr{C}}(F,k)\otimes_{k}{\mathscr{C}}(G,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(F\times G,k)

of (
5.24.1
)
is obviously an isomorphism of ring-objects of
{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
, so we are done.

\mathit{2}.
Follows from
\mathit{1}
taking
{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C,C^{\prime}}
, where
C,C^{\prime}
are compact opens in
X,Y
, respectively, and using Proposition
2.15
.

\mathit{3}.
We obtain the morphism
{\mathcal{L}}^{(X,Y)}
of (
5.24.4
) as follows.
We have a natural morphism

\begin{split}{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)&\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}{\mathscr{C}}(Y,k)={\rm lim}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}{\rm lim}_{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}(Y)}{\mathscr{C}}(C^{\prime},k)\xrightarrow{\ \eqref{tensfunct2}\ }\\
&{\rm lim}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X),C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}(Y)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}{\mathscr{C}}(C^{\prime},k)\;.\end{split}

Since

{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}{\mathscr{C}}(C^{\prime},k)={\mathscr{C}}(C\times C^{\prime},k)

by
\mathit{1}
, the previous formula continues into

={\rm lim}_{C\times C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}(X\times Y)}{\mathscr{C}}(C\times C^{\prime},k)={\mathscr{C}}(X\times Y,k)\;,

and the composition is
{\mathcal{L}}^{(X,Y)}
of (
5.24.4
).
The morphism
{\mathcal{L}}^{(X,Y)}
is an isomorphism because it is the completion of the isomorphism (
5.24.3
) in the topology of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(Y,k)
(resp. of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X\times Y,k)
)
as a subspace of
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}{\mathscr{C}}(Y,k)
(resp.
{\mathscr{C}}(X\times Y,k)
).

\mathit{4}.
From (
5.8.4
) we see that
for
D
discrete
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)
is simply
k^{D}
equipped with the naive canonical
topology; so the existence of the morphism (
5.24.5
) and the fact that it is injective is clear.

\mathit{5}.

We obtain the morphism in (
5.24.6
) by taking colimits of the filtered inductive system of injective morphisms
\{{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm unif}^{(D,E)}\}_{\pi_{D}\in{\mathscr{J}},\pi_{E}\in{\mathscr{H}}}
(
5.24.5
) in
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
, and using Proposition
2.15
.
To show that the map
{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm unif}^{(X,Y)}
of (
5.24.6
) is injective we use Corollary
5.22
by which

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k/I)={\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k/I)\;,

where
{\rm colim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}^{\rm u}
equals set-theoretically
{\rm colim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}
in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k/I}
.
By (
5.24.5
), for any
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
, we get injective morphisms in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k/I}

{\mathcal{L}}^{(D,E)}_{\rm unif}:{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k/I)\otimes_{k/I}{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm unif}(E,k/I)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D\times E,k/I)\;.

Since
{\mathscr{J}}
is filtered, the functor
{\rm colim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}
preserves injectivity in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k/I}
, so we get an injective map

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k/I)\otimes_{k/I}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((Y,\Theta_{\mathscr{H}}),k/I)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X\times Y,\Theta_{{\mathscr{J}}\times{\mathscr{H}}}),k/I)\;.

We now apply
{\rm lim}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}
to the previous inductive system of morphisms and use Proposition
2.15
to get the result.

∎

Remark 5.25
.

Notice that
{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm unif}^{(X,Y)}
in (
5.24.6
) is
not an isomorphism in general.
This fact is already visible in case of (
5.24.5
) when
k
is discrete and
D
,
E
are discrete but infinite.
In fact in the latter case,
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)=(k^{D})^{\rm dis\,}
,
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(E,k)=(k^{E})^{\rm dis\,}
, and
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D\times E,k)=(k^{D\times E})^{\rm dis\,}
are discrete so that

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(E,k)={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)\otimes_{k}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(E,k)
. But if, say,

D=E
, the characteristic function of the diagonal does not belong to the image of
{\mathcal{L}}^{(D,E)}_{\rm unif}
. Of course, if
k^{D}
,
k^{E}
are endowed with their natural (product) topology, then the map

k^{D}\widehat{\otimes}_{k}^{\rm u}k^{E}\xrightarrow{\ \ }k^{D\times E}\;\;,\;\;(a_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\times(b_{\beta})_{\beta}\longmapsto(a_{\alpha}b_{\beta})_{\alpha,\beta}

is an isomorphism and we are not contradicting (
5.24.4
).

Remark 5.26
.

Let
X
(resp.
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
)
be a
td
space (resp. a uniform
td
space).
It follows from
Proposition
5.24
that for
?=\emptyset,{\rm acs}
(resp. for
?=\emptyset,{\rm acs},{\rm unif}
), the diagonal map
\Delta_{X}:X\longrightarrow X\times X
induces an associative and commutative product
(f,g)\longmapsto\mu_{?}(f,g)
, composite of the maps

{\mathcal{C}}_{?}(X,k)\times{\mathcal{C}}_{?}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ \widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}\ }{\mathcal{C}}_{?}(X,k)\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}{\mathcal{C}}_{?}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ {\mathcal{L}}^{(X,X)}_{?}\ }{\mathscr{C}}_{?}(X\times X,k)\xrightarrow{\ \Delta_{X}^{\ast}\ }{\mathscr{C}}_{?}(X,k)\;,

with the required properties (in particular uniform continuity in
(f,g)
for the product uniformity).
Then
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
(resp.
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
)
is a commutative unital
k
-algebra object of the category
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
(resp. and of the category
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
), with identity the constant function
1
on
X
. If
X
is a metrizable
td
-space, then
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
is a
k
-algebra object of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
.
The product of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
is also associative and commutative, but
fails to define a unital
k
-algebra object of
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
because the constant function
1
is not in
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)
unless
X
is compact.
We recall that

{\mathcal{L}}^{(X,X)}
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{(X,X)}_{\rm acs}
are isomorphisms, while
{\mathcal{L}}^{(X,X)}_{\rm unif}
is only an injective morphism.

6
Measures with values in
k
on
td
-spaces via duality

We will now construct functors on the categories
td-{\mathcal{S}}
and
td-{\mathcal{U}}
with values in the category
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}
, dual, as much as possible, to the functors constructed in section
5
.
Let
X
be a
td
-space (resp.
X=(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
be a
td
-uniform space,
for a cofiltered sub-projective system
{\mathscr{J}}
of
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
).
For the
td
-space
X
we have defined 2 types of rings of continuous
k
-valued functions on
X
, namely
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\subset{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
. For the
td
-uniform space
X=(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
we defined a further
k
-algebra
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
of uniformly continuous functions situated between
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)
and
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
. They are related by sequence
5.15.2
.

6.1
Case of
X=C
a Stone space

We recall
that in this case
all
morphisms in (
5.15.2
) are isomorphisms and that
(
5.18.1
) holds.
We then introduce both the strong dual

(6.0.1)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)&:={\mathscr{C}}(C,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)}k^{(F,{\rm u})}\\
&={\rm lim}^{\rm can}_{F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)}k^{(F,{\rm u})}\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\end{split}

and the weak dual

(6.0.2)

{\mathscr{D}}(C,k):={\mathscr{C}}(C,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\rm lim}_{F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)}k^{(F,{\rm u})}\in{\rm lim}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{k}

of
{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)
. Notice that, if
C
is metrizable, then
{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)
is a sequential limit of objects of
{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
, hence is an object of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\omega,\rm u}_{k}
.

The natural morphism

{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)

is bijective by (
2.6.5
), so that we may regard the underlying
k
-module of (
6.0.1
) and of
(
6.0.2
) as the same
k
-module
{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)^{\rm for}={\rm Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}}({\mathscr{C}}(C,k),k)
of
measures on
(the Stone space)
C
equipped with the
strong
(resp.
weak
) topology. By Lemma
5.6
locally constant functions
C\longrightarrow k
are dense in
{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)
, so a
k
-valued measure on
C
may be viewed as a map

(6.0.3)

\mu:\Sigma(C)={\mathcal{K}}(C)\longrightarrow k

such that if
C_{1},\dots,C_{n}
are a finite family of disjoint clopen subsets of
C
,

(6.0.4)

\mu(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}C_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu(C_{i})\;.

(We are using the fact that, for
M,N\in{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}
, any
k
-linear map
M\to N
is continuous for the naive canonical topology.)

Remark 6.1
.

Obviously, a measure
\mu
on the Stone space
C
is determined by the restriction of
(
6.0.3
) to any basis of the topology of
C
contained in
\Sigma(C)
.

A fundamental system of open
k
-submodules for
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)
is given by the family

\{V_{I}\}_{I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)}
where

(6.1.1)

V_{I}=\{\mu\in{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)^{\rm for}\,|\,\mu(U)\in I\;,\;\forall\;U\in\Sigma(C)\;\}\;.

For
\pi_{F}:C\longrightarrow F
in
{\mathscr{Q}}(C)
, we identify
F
with the corresponding clopen partition
{\mathscr{P}}_{F}=\{\pi_{F}^{-1}(x)\}_{x\in F}
of
C
. Then, a fundamental system of open
k
-submodules for
{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)
is given by the family of
k
-submodules

(6.1.2)

V_{F,I}:=\{\mu\in{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)^{\rm for}\,|\,\mu(U)\in I\;,\;\forall\;U\in{\mathscr{P}}_{F}\;\}

indexed by
F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)
and
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
.
Notice that the natural maps

(6.1.3)

\bigoplus_{x\in C}k\delta_{x}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)\xrightarrow{\ (1:1)\ }{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)\;,

where
\delta_{x}
is interpreted as the Dirac mass at
x
, are obviously injective. It is also clear that the
composite map has dense set-theoretic image.
We
may then explicitly describe
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)
and
{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)
.

Proposition 6.2
.

The natural map

\bigoplus_{x\in C}k\delta_{x}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)\;,

is injective and

V_{I}\bigcap\bigoplus_{x\in C}k\delta_{x}=I\bigoplus_{x\in C}k\delta_{x}=\bigoplus_{x\in C}I\delta_{x}

so that

(6.2.1)

{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)={\bigoplus}_{x\in C}^{\rm u}k\delta_{x}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;V_{I}=I{\bigoplus}_{x\in C}^{\rm u}k\delta_{x}={\bigoplus}_{x\in C}^{\rm u}I\delta_{x}\;.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)
and
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
.
Then

{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)^{\rm for}=({\bigoplus}_{x\in C}^{\rm u}k\delta_{x})^{\rm for}

and
{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)
identifies with (resp. the completion of) the
k
-module
({\bigoplus}_{x\in C}^{\rm u}k\delta_{x})^{\rm for}
(resp.
\bigoplus_{x\in C}k\delta_{x}
) equipped with the
k
-linear topology with fundamental system of open
k
-submodules
\{V_{F,I}\}_{F,I}
(resp.
\{V^{\prime}_{F,I}\}_{F,I}
), where

(6.3.1)

V_{F,I}=\{\sum_{x\in C}a_{x}\delta_{x}\in{\bigoplus}_{x\in C}^{\rm u}k\delta_{x}\,|\,\sum_{x\in P}a_{x}\in I\;,\;\forall\;P\in{\mathscr{P}}_{F}\,\}

(resp.

(6.3.2)

\begin{split}V^{\prime}_{F,I}:=&V_{F,I}\bigcap{\bigoplus}_{x\in C}k\delta_{x}=\\
&\{\sum_{x\in C}a_{x}\delta_{x}\in{\bigoplus}_{x\in C}k\delta_{x}\,|\,\sum_{x\in P}a_{x}\in I\;,\;\forall\;P\in{\mathscr{P}}_{F}\,\}\;\mbox{)\;.}\end{split}

Remark 6.4
.

Notice that the expression
\sum_{x\in P}a_{x}
in (
6.3.1
) represents an unconditionally convergent series in
k
, while in (
6.3.2
) it is a finite sum.

Corollary 6.5
.

{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)
is precisely the space of
k
-valued measures on
C
in the sense of (
6.0.3
) and (
6.0.4
), equipped with the
k
-linear topology with fundamental system of open
k
-submodules
\{V_{F,I}\}_{F,I}
, for
F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)
and
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
, defined in (
6.1.2
). Instead,
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)={\mathscr{D}}(C,k)^{\rm can}
, is the space of
k
-valued measures on
C
equipped with its naive
k
-canonical topology.

Remark 6.6
.

It follows from Corollary
3.13
that the transpose of the map
{\mathcal{L}}^{(C,C^{\prime})}
of
(
5.24.2
) in

\mathit{1}
of Proposition
5.24
gives a natural isomorphism
of
k
-coalgebra objects of
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}

(6.6.1)

{\mathcal{L}}^{(C,C^{\prime})}_{\mathscr{D}}:{\mathscr{D}}^{!}(C\times C^{\prime},k)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }{\mathscr{D}}^{!}(C,k)\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}{\mathscr{D}}^{!}(C^{\prime},k)

for
!=\emptyset,\circ
.
On the other hand, it follows from Remark
6.1
that, for Stone spaces
C,C^{\prime}
, there is a natural map

{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)\times{\mathscr{D}}(C^{\prime},k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathscr{D}}(C\times C^{\prime},k)

taking
(\mu,\nu)
to the measure
\mu\times\nu
on
C\times C^{\prime}
such that

(\mu\times\nu)(U\times V)=\mu(U)\nu(V)\;\;,\;\;\forall\>U\in\Sigma(C)\,,\,V\in\Sigma(C^{\prime})\;.

The map
\mu\otimes\nu\longmapsto\mu\times\nu
is the inverse of
{\mathcal{L}}^{(C,C^{\prime})}_{\mathscr{D}}
. For any
x\in C
and
y\in C^{\prime}
,
\delta_{x}\otimes\delta_{y}\longmapsto\delta_{(x,y)}
, so
the fact that (
6.6.1
) is an isomorphism also follows from the explicit description of Proposition
6.3
.

We observe that by (
6.0.2
) (resp. (
6.0.1
)) the duality rule
(
3.6.3
) (resp. (
3.6.4
)) gives, taking into account (
5.5.1
),

(6.6.2)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\mathscr{D}}(C,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}=({\mathscr{D}}_{\rm strong}(C,k))^{\prime}_{\rm weak}&=({\mathscr{D}}_{\rm strong}(C,k))^{\prime}_{\rm strong}=\\
{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{F\in{\mathscr{Q}}(C)}k^{(F,{\rm u})}={\mathscr{C}}(C,k)&\;.\end{split}

Definition 6.7
.

When
C
is a Stone space, we are more interested in
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm weak}(C,k)
, so we simply denote it by
{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)
and understand that it carries the weak topology. That topology will be called the
natural topology
of
{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)
.
We call
{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)
the
k
-module of
measures on

C
with values in
k
. We will call instead
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)
the
k
-canonical module
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm strong}(C,k)={\mathscr{D}}(C,k)^{\rm can}
.

6.2
Case of
X=D
a discrete space

If
X=D
is discrete and the uniformity on
D
is understood to be the discrete uniformity, (
5.15.2
) becomes (
5.18.2
), which we now dualize.

By the second line of formulas in Corollary
3.7
, the strong and weak duals coincide for
{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)
. We set

(6.7.1)

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k):={\mathscr{C}}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\mathscr{C}}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}\cong{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}

and call it the
k
-module of (
k
-valued) measures
with almost compact support
(on the discrete space
D
).

The strong and weak duals
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}
and
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}
of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)

are

(6.7.2)

{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D,k):={\prod}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;{\mathscr{D}}(D,k):={\prod}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}\;,

respectively.
We observe immediately that the second and third lines of formulas in Corollary
3.7
imply

(6.7.3)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}&={\mathscr{D}}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\mathscr{D}}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}=\\
&{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\;.\end{split}

We are mainly interested in

(6.7.4)

{\mathscr{D}}(D,k)={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\prod}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}\;.

We call
{\mathscr{D}}(D,k)
the
k
-module of (
k
-valued)
measures
(on the discrete space
D
). From the dense injection
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\subset{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)
in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm u}_{k}

we obtain by (strong and weak) duality the two injections

(6.7.5)

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D,k)\xrightarrow{\ (1:1)\ }{\mathscr{D}}(D,k)

whose composite has dense image.
Explicitly they are

(6.7.6)

\begin{split}&{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)={\mathscr{C}}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\mathscr{C}}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}\longrightarrow\\
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D,k)=&{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\prod}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}\xrightarrow{\ (1:1)\ }{\mathscr{D}}(D,k)={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\prod}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}\;.\end{split}

By (weak and strong) duality again, we re-obtain the injections

(6.7.7)

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathscr{C}}^{\circ}(D,k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)\;.

Explicitly they are

\begin{split}&{\mathscr{D}}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}=({\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D,k))^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{x\in D}k\chi_{x}={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }\\
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}&={\prod}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{x\in D}k\chi_{x}={\mathscr{C}}^{\circ}(D,k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\prod}_{x\in D}k\chi_{x}={\mathscr{C}}(D,k)\;.\end{split}

Let
{\mathscr{F}}(D)
(resp.
{\mathfrak{P}}(D)
) be the set of finite (resp. of all) subsets of
D
. Then
{\mathscr{D}}(D,k)={\prod}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}
(resp.

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm strong}(D,k)={\prod}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}
) may be described as
the family of functions
\mu:{\mathscr{F}}(D)\longrightarrow k
such that, for any
X\in{\mathscr{F}}(D)
,

(6.7.8)

\mu(X)=\sum_{x\in X}\mu(\{x\})\;.

Instead
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)={\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}
is the family of functions
\mu:{\mathfrak{P}}(D)\longrightarrow k

with the following two properties :

1.

for any
I\in{\mathcal{P}}(k)
, there exists a finite subset
F_{I}\subset D
, such that

\mu(x)\in I\;,\;\;\forall\;x\in D-F_{I}\;;

2.

for any
X\in{\mathfrak{P}}(D)
, the series
\sum_{x\in X}\mu(\{x\})
is unconditionally convergent to
\mu(X)
.

Then
{\mathscr{D}}(D,k)
(resp.
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D,k)
,
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)
) is equipped with the topology of simple (resp. of uniform) convergence
on (the singletons of)
D
. We now observe that
the strong and the weak dual of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)={\prod}_{x\in D}^{\rm can}k\chi_{x}
coincide and also coincide with the strong and the weak dual of
{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)={\prod}_{x\in D}k\chi_{x}
. We set

(6.7.9)

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k):={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\mathscr{C}}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\mathscr{C}}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}

so that

(6.7.10)

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)={\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)={\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.

By the first line of formulas in Corollary
3.7
,

(6.7.11)

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)\;\;,\;\;{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\mathscr{C}}(D,k)\;.

Moreover, (
5.18.2
) may be reinterpreted as

(6.7.12)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}&(D,k)={\mathscr{D}}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\mathscr{D}}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}=\\
&{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}\;\;\;\longrightarrow\\
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)=&{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}\;\;\;\longrightarrow\;\;\;{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)={\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}\;.\end{split}

We also have

(6.7.13)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)&={\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm strong}(D,k)={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}\\
\longrightarrow&{\mathscr{D}}(D,k)={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}\end{split}

corresponding to the natural maps

{\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}\longrightarrow{\prod}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}\longrightarrow{\prod}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}\;.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
D
be a discrete topological space. Then

1.

{\mathscr{D}}(D,k)={\prod}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}
is the space of functions
\mu:{\mathscr{F}}(D)\longrightarrow k
such that, for any
X\in{\mathscr{F}}(D)
, (
6.7.8
) holds. It is equipped with the topology of simple convergence
on the singletons of
D
.

2.

{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D,k)={\prod}^{\rm can}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}
coincides set-theoretically with
{\mathscr{D}}(D,k)
.
It is equipped with its naive
k
-canonical topology
i.e.
with the topology of uniform convergence
on the singletons of
D
.

3.

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)={\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)={\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}
is the space of functions

(6.8.1)

\mu:{\mathfrak{P}}(D)\longrightarrow k

such that for any
X\subset D
, the
X
-series
(
6.7.8
)
is unconditionally convergent in
k
to
\mu(X)
. It is equipped with the topology of uniform convergence
on
{\mathfrak{P}}(D)
which makes it a (closed) subspace of
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D,k)
.

Proof.

Clear.
∎

6.3
Functoriality of functions and measures

6.3.1
Restriction of functions and extension by
0
of measures on Stone spaces

For
C\subset C^{\prime}
compact opens in
X
we have “restriction” maps

(6.8.2)

\begin{split}\rho_{C,C^{\prime}}:{\mathscr{C}}(C^{\prime},k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\\
f&\longmapsto f_{|C}\end{split}

and, by ?-duality, for
?={\rm weak},{\rm strong}
, “extension by 0” maps for measures, for
!=\emptyset,\circ
, respectively,

(6.8.3)

\begin{split}j_{C,C^{\prime}}:{\mathscr{D}}^{!}(C,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}^{!}(C^{\prime},k)\\
\mu&\longmapsto j_{C,C^{\prime}}(\mu)\;,\end{split}

where
j_{C,C^{\prime}}(\mu)(f)=\mu(f_{|C})
, for any
f\in{\mathscr{C}}(C^{\prime},k)
. In particular,
j_{C,C^{\prime}}(\delta_{x})=\delta_{x}
, for any
x\in C
.

Since

{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)={\rm lim}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}({\mathscr{C}}(C,k),\rho_{C,C^{\prime}})

the following definition makes sense.

Definition 6.9
.

We set

(6.9.1)

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k):={\mathscr{C}}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}({\mathscr{D}}(C,k),j_{C,C^{\prime}})

and

(6.9.2)

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}^{\circ}(X,k):={\mathscr{C}}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}({\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k),j_{C,C^{\prime}})\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.

By application of
{\rm lim}_{C}
to (
6.8.2
) we obtain

(6.9.3)

\rho_{C}:{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\;\;\mbox{(restriction)}

and from (
6.9.1
) (resp. (
6.16.6
)) we get the weak (resp. strong) dual
extension by
0
map

(6.9.4)

\begin{split}j_{C}:{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\;\;\mbox{(weak case)}\;,\\
\mbox{(resp. \;}\;j_{C}:{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\;\;\mbox{(strong case)\;)}\;.\end{split}

6.3.2
Extension by
0
of functions and restriction of measures on Stone spaces

We have an “extension by 0” map for functions

(6.9.5)

\begin{split}i_{C,C^{\prime}}:{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(C^{\prime},k)\\
f&\longmapsto i_{C,C^{\prime}}(f)\;,\end{split}

and its weak transpose “restriction” map for measures

(6.9.6)

\begin{split}\sigma_{C,C^{\prime}}:{\mathscr{D}}(C^{\prime},k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)\\
\mu&\longmapsto\sigma_{C,C^{\prime}}(\mu)\;,\end{split}

such that
\sigma_{C,C^{\prime}}(\mu)(f)=\mu(i_{C,C^{\prime}}(f))
, for any
\mu\in{\mathscr{D}}(C^{\prime},k)
and
f\in{\mathcal{C}}(C,k)
.

Definition 6.10
.

We set

(6.10.1)

{\mathscr{D}}(X,k):={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\rm lim}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}({\mathscr{D}}(C,k),\sigma_{C,C^{\prime}})\in{\rm lim}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{k}

and obtain

(6.10.2)

\begin{split}i_{C}:{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\;\;\mbox{(extension by $0$)}\;,\\
\sigma_{C}:{\mathscr{D}}(X,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)\;\;\mbox{(restriction)}\;,\end{split}

weakly dual to each other. Similarly, we set

(6.10.3)

{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(X,k):={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}({\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k),\sigma_{C,C^{\prime}})\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}

and then
\sigma_{C}
in (
6.10.2
) is replaced by the restriction map

\sigma_{C}:{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(X,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)

where
i_{C}
and
\sigma_{C}
are strongly dual to each other.

Notice that, for any
x\in X
,

(6.10.4)

\sigma_{C}(\delta_{x})=\begin{cases}\delta_{x}&\text{if }x\in C\\
&\\
0&\text{if }x\in X-C\;.\end{cases}

6.3.3
Pull-back of functions and push-forward of measures on discrete spaces

For
\pi_{D}:X\to D
and
\pi_{D^{\prime}}:X\to D^{\prime}
in
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
and the morphism
\pi_{D,D^{\prime}}:D^{\prime}\to D
in

{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
, we have the “pull-back” maps of functions

(6.10.5)

\begin{split}\pi^{\ast}_{D,D^{\prime}}:{\mathscr{C}}_{?}(D,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{?}(D^{\prime},k)\\
f&\longmapsto f\circ\pi_{D,D^{\prime}}\end{split}

for
?=\emptyset,{\rm acs}
, and the weak transpose of each of them. Let
!={\rm acs}
if
?=\emptyset
and

!=\emptyset
if
?={\rm acs}
.
We get “push-forward” maps of measures

(6.10.6)

h_{D,D^{\prime}}:{\mathscr{D}}_{!}(D^{\prime},k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{!}(D,k)

such that
h_{D,D^{\prime}}(\mu)(f)=\mu(f\circ\pi_{D,D^{\prime}})
for any
\mu\in{\mathscr{D}}_{!}(D^{\prime},k)
and
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{?}(D,k)
. The
push-forward of a measure
\mu
attributes a
\mu
-weight to each fiber by adding the
\mu
-weights of all points in each fiber :

(6.10.7)

\mu\longmapsto\sum_{x\in D}\mu(\pi^{-1}_{D,D^{\prime}}(x))\,\delta_{x}\;.

By application of
{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D}
and
{\rm lim}_{D}
, respectively, we obtain,

1.

in case
?=\emptyset
,
!={\rm acs}

(6.10.8)

\begin{split}\pi_{D}^{\ast}:{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\;\;\mbox{(pull-back of functions)}\;,\\
h_{D}:{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\;\;\mbox{(push-forward of measures)}\;,\end{split}

weakly dual to each other;

2.

in case
?={\rm acs}
,
!=\emptyset

(6.10.9)

\begin{split}\pi_{D}^{\ast}:{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\\
h_{D}:{\mathscr{D}}(X,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}(D,k)\;,\end{split}

weakly dual to each other.

The map
h_{D}
in both (
6.10.8
) and (
6.10.9
) is given by

(6.10.10)

\mu\longmapsto\sum_{x\in D}\mu(\pi^{-1}_{D}(x))\,\delta_{x}\;,

which makes sense because
\pi^{-1}_{D}(x)\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, for any
x\in D
, so that
\mu(\pi^{-1}_{D}(x))
is defined.

6.4
The case of
X
a general
td
-space

6.4.1
Measures with and without support conditions

Remark 6.11
.

For
X=D
discrete, comparing (
6.9.1
) and (
6.16.6
) with (
6.7.1
) we see that

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)={\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.

For
X=C
a Stone space,
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(C,k)={\mathscr{D}}(C,k)
while
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(C,k)={\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)
.

We have

(6.11.1)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}=&\left({\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}({\mathscr{D}}(C,k),j_{C,C^{\prime}})\right)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}=\\
{\rm lim}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}&{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\rm lim}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)={\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\;,\end{split}

where we have used the biduality formula (
6.6.2
) for the Stone space
C
. Similarly, we also have

(6.11.2)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}=&\left({\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}({\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k),j_{C,C^{\prime}})\right)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}=\\
{\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}&{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\rm lim}^{\rm can}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)={\mathscr{C}}^{\circ}(X,k)\;,\end{split}

where again we have applied (
6.6.2
) to
C
.
In the same way we also check that

(6.11.3)

{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}\;.

We also observe for completeness that from (
3.6.4
) we deduce

(6.11.4)

\begin{split}({\mathscr{C}}^{\circ}(X,k))^{\prime}_{\rm strong}&=({\rm lim}^{\rm can}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k))^{\prime}_{\rm strong}=\\
{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}({\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k),j_{C,C^{\prime}})&={\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\;.\end{split}

Morphisms
C\longrightarrow X
in
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
and
\pi_{D}:X\longrightarrow D
in
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
determine sequences

(6.11.5)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{D}^{\ast}\ }&{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ \rho_{C}\ }{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\\
{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)\xrightarrow{\ j_{C}\ }&{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ h_{D}\ }{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\;,\end{split}

(resp.

(6.11.6)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{C}}^{\circ}(D,k)\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{D}^{\ast}\ }&{\mathscr{C}}^{\circ}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ \rho_{C}\ }{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\\
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)\xrightarrow{\ j_{C}\ }&{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ h_{D}\ }{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\;\mbox{)}\;,\end{split}

weakly (resp. strongly) dual to each other.

Since we have the two equalities

{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}({\mathscr{C}}(D,k),\pi_{D,D^{\prime}}^{\ast})={\rm lim}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}({\mathscr{C}}(C,k),\rho_{C,C^{\prime}})

we conclude that we have at the same time

(6.11.7)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)=&{\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}({\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k),h_{D,D^{\prime}})\\
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X,k)=&{\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{D\in{\mathscr{Q}}(X)}({\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k),h_{D,D^{\prime}})\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\end{split}

and

(6.11.8)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)=&{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}({\mathscr{D}}(C,k),j_{C,C^{\prime}})\\
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X,k)=&{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}({\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k),h_{D,D^{\prime}})\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.\end{split}

Corollary 6.12
.

The canonical morphism

{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)

induced by the bijective morphisms
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)\xrightarrow{\ (1:1)\ }{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)
, for any
C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, is bijective. Therefore,

(6.12.1)

{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X,k)={\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)^{\rm can}\;.

Morphisms
C\longrightarrow X
in
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
and
X\longrightarrow D
in
{\mathscr{Q}}(X)
determine, by (
6.10.1
), (
6.10.2
),
(
6.9.4
) and (
6.10.9
),
sequences

(6.12.2)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{D}^{\ast}\ }{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)&\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ \rho_{C}\ }{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\xrightarrow{\ i_{C}\ }{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\\
{\mathscr{D}}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ \sigma_{C}\ }{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)&\xrightarrow{\ j_{C}\ }{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ h_{D}\ }{\mathscr{D}}(D,k)\;,\end{split}

weakly dual to each other.

Proposition 6.13
.

For any
td
-space
X
, we have

(6.13.1)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}=&{\mathscr{D}}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}=\\
&{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\end{split}

Proof.

From Definition
6.10
it follows that

(6.13.2)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}&={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}=\\
&{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\end{split}

and

(6.13.3)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}&={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}=\\
&{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.\end{split}

Similarly,

(6.13.4)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}&={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{D}}(C,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\\
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(X,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}&={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(C,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)}{\mathscr{C}}(C,k)\;.\end{split}

∎

6.4.2
Uniform measures

Let
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})={\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}(X,\{\pi_{D}\})
be a neat
td
-uniform space,
as in (
4.28.1
).
Then we define

(6.13.5)

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k):={\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}({\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k),h_{D,D^{\prime}})

and

(6.13.6)

{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k):={\rm lim}^{\rm can}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}({\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k),h_{D,D^{\prime}})\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.

The elements of
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
will be called
uniform
(
k
-valued)
measures
on

(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
. We recall that, by (
6.7.10
),
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)
may be replaced by
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)
in
(
6.13.5
) so that, by
(
6.11.7
), we deduce a morphism

(6.13.7)

\mbox{(}\;{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ (1:1)\ }\;\mbox{)}\;{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\xrightarrow{\ {\rm inc}\ }{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)

with dense set-theoretic image obtained from the push-forwards of measures
h_{D}:{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\to{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)
, for
D\in{\mathscr{J}}
(
6.10.8
).

Since
(X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}})
is neat, the
k
-linear map

(6.13.8)

{\bigoplus}_{x\in X}k\delta_{x}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)={\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\bigoplus}^{\rm u}_{d\in D}k\delta_{d}

sending any
x\in X
to the projective system
\{\delta_{\pi_{D}(x)}\}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}
is an
injection with dense image.
So that
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
contains a copy of the
k
-module freely generated by
\{\delta_{x}\}_{x\in X}
.

We then have

(6.13.9)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}&((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}=\\
&{\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)={\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)\end{split}

(6.13.10)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}&((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}={\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}=\\
&{\rm lim}^{\square,{\rm u}}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)={\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)\end{split}

(6.13.11)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}&((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)^{\prime}_{\rm strong}=({\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}({\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k),h_{D,D^{\prime}}))^{\prime}_{\rm strong}=\\
&{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}({\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k),\pi_{D,D^{\prime}}^{\ast})={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)\in{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}\;.\end{split}

(6.13.12)

\begin{split}{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}&((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)^{\prime}_{\rm weak}=({\rm lim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k))^{\prime}_{\rm weak}=\\
&{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)={\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\;.\end{split}

Only the last formula needs justification. For any
D\in{\mathscr{J}}
, we have a natural bijective morphism,

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)
and therefore taking (filtered) set-theoretic colimits a bijection in
{{\mathcal{M}}}od_{k}

(6.13.13)

{\rm colim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\rm colim}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)\;.

The natural morphism

{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)={\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)

comes from completion of the l.h.s. of (
6.13.13
) in the topology of uniform convergence on
X
, which produces
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)
, and of the r.h.s. of (
6.13.13
), or equivalently of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)^{\rm for}
, in the topology of uniform convergence on the family

\{\pi_{D}^{-1}(F)\,|\,D\in{\mathscr{J}}\;,\;F\in{\mathcal{F}}(D)\;\}\;.

But the latter family if cofinal with
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, and the completion of

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)^{\rm for}
in that topology is therefore
{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)
.
So
completion of (
6.13.13
) produces an isomorphism

{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{D\in{\mathscr{J}}}{\mathscr{C}}(D,k)={\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\;,

as claimed.

Remark 6.14
.

The integration pairing
(\mu,f)\longmapsto\mu\circ f:=\int_{X}f(x)d\mu(x)
on

(6.14.1)

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)\times{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm unif}((X,\Theta_{\mathscr{J}}),k)\longrightarrow k

is weak-left perfect by (
6.13.9
) and strong-right perfect (
6.13.11
). The same integration pairing for

(6.14.2)

{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\times{\mathscr{C}}(X,k)\longrightarrow k\;\;,\;\;(\mu,f)\longmapsto\int_{X}f(x)d\mu(x)\;.

is weak-perfect.

6.4.3
Coproduct of measures

We deduce from (
6.7.6
) and (
6.7.10
) that, for
D
and
E
discrete, the

k
-linear isomorphism

(6.14.3)

{\bigoplus}_{x\in D,y\in E}k\delta_{(x,y)}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }{\bigoplus}_{x\in D}k\delta_{x}\otimes_{k}{\bigoplus}_{y\in E}k\delta_{y}

extends to an isomorphism

(6.14.4)

{\mathscr{D}}_{?}(D\times E,k)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }{\mathscr{D}}_{?}(D,k)\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}{\mathscr{D}}_{?}(E,k)\;,

for
?={\rm acs},\emptyset
. Notice that the morphism

(6.14.5)

{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D\times E,k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D,k)\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(E,k)

is injective but not necessarily an isomorphism.

We define a
k
-coalgebra structure on
{\mathscr{D}}_{?}(D,k)
via the
k
-linear continuous coproduct

(6.14.6)

{\mathbb{P}}^{(D)}_{\mathscr{D}}:{\mathscr{D}}_{?}(D,k)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{?}(D\times D,k)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }{\mathscr{D}}_{?}(D,k)\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}{\mathscr{D}}_{?}(D,k)

sending
\delta_{x}\longmapsto\delta_{(x,x)}\longmapsto\delta_{x}\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}\delta_{x}
, for any
x\in D
. Similarly on

{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}(D,k)
.
On
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)
we also have the (
k
-linear continuous) augmentation

\varepsilon^{(D)}_{\mathscr{D}}:{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)\to k\;,

sending
\delta_{x}
to 1, for any
x\in D
, so that
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(D,k)
(
={\mathscr{D}}_{\rm unif}(D,k)
) is a (unital)
k
-coalgebra object of
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
.

By Proposition
2.15
, the
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
-isomorphisms
{\mathcal{L}}^{(C,C^{\prime})}_{\mathscr{D}}
of (
6.6.1
), for
!=\circ
,
induce by application of the functor
{\rm colim}^{\rm u}_{C\in{\mathcal{K}}(X),C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}(Y)}

an isomorphism

(6.14.7)

{\mathcal{L}}^{(X,Y)}_{\mathscr{D}}:{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X\times Y,k)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(Y,k)

in
{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm can}_{k}
.
For
!=\emptyset
we
a priori
only obtain a morphism (
2.14.1
)

(6.14.8)

{\mathcal{L}}^{(X,Y)}_{\mathscr{D}}:{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X\times Y,k)\xrightarrow{\ \ }{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(Y,k)\;.

It follows from Corollary
6.12
that
{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}{\mathscr{D}}^{\circ}_{\rm acs}(Y,k)
is a dense subset of
{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(X,k)\widehat{\otimes}^{\rm u}_{k}{\mathscr{D}}_{\rm acs}(Y,k)
. So, the morphism (
6.14.8
) is obtained by completion of
the
k
